Furthermore, the demonstration of improved inference robustness is a valuable additional benefit, potentially leading to more fault-tolerant or efficient inference deployment.  The performance gains are significant and have practical implications for reducing the cost and time required to train LLMs. However, some considerations significantly affect the evaluation:

    *   **Heterogeneity**: The benefit claims appear to be specifically for heterogeneous network settings, although it is hard to evaluate that in the provided homogeneous environment (they simulate heterogenous latencies). What are the theoretical or empirical benefits of using SkipPipe in homogeneous GPU clusters that are ubiquitous nowadays.

    *   **Scale:** The experiments are conducted up to 8B models and 20 nodes, so it remains unverified whether SkipPipe scales effectively to larger models trained on clusters with hundreds or thousands of GPUs, which is often the norm in contemporary LLM development.

    *   **Convergence:** While the paper indicates that there is "no convergence degradation", it would be useful to compare in more detail the learning curves obtained with SkipPipe and traditional pipelining, or even show loss variance.

    *   **Path Complexity:**  Finding optimal or near-optimal paths, is an NP-hard problem, so the authors use heuristics. The heuristics, though described in detail in the appendix, are somewhat ad-hoc, and there is a lack of theoretical guarantees on their effectiveness. The authors have given a thorough description of the path selection algorithm, however, it is quite complicated.

    *   **Limitations and Assumptions**:  The method makes assumptions about per-layer computational intensity, and bandwidth heterogeneity.

*   **Clarity and Reproducibility:** The paper is reasonably well-written, but the detailed description of the path scheduling algorithm can be difficult to follow. The provision of the code is vital for the reproducibility and further development of the framework. Also, code availability facilitates a deeper understanding of the heuristics employed, which may give rise to the possibility of making them more transparent.

Overall, the paper presents a worthwhile contribution by introducing a novel framework for distributed LLM training. While the experimental evaluation could be strengthened through an exploration of model scale and cluster size and a clear exposition of the limitations and assumptions, the approach shows promising results and deserves recognition. Given the current environment, it is likely to make a relatively small but solid impact to the field.
**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM-driven Effective Knowledge Tracing by Integrating Dual-channel Difficulty
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19915v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahui Cen, Jianghao Lin, Weizhong Xuan, Dong Zhou, Jin Chen, Aimin Yang, Yongmei Zhou
- **Abstract**: Knowledge Tracing (KT) is a fundamental technology in intelligent tutoring systems used to simulate changes in students' knowledge state during learning, track personalized knowledge mastery, and predict performance. However, current KT models face three major challenges: (1) When encountering new questions, models face cold-start problems due to sparse interaction records, making precise modeling difficult; (2) Traditional models only use historical interaction records for student personalization modeling, unable to accurately track individual mastery levels, resulting in unclear personalized modeling; (3) The decision-making process is opaque to educators, making it challenging for them to understand model judgments. To address these challenges, we propose a novel Dual-channel Difficulty-aware Knowledge Tracing (DDKT) framework that utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) for subjective difficulty assessment, while integrating difficulty bias-aware algorithms and student mastery algorithms for precise difficulty measurement. Our framework introduces three key innovations: (1) Difficulty Balance Perception Sequence (DBPS) - students' subjective perceptions combined with objective difficulty, measuring gaps between LLM-assessed difficulty, mathematical-statistical difficulty, and students' subjective perceived difficulty through attention mechanisms; (2) Difficulty Mastery Ratio (DMR) - precise modeling of student mastery levels through different difficulty zones; (3) Knowledge State Update Mechanism - implementing personalized knowledge acquisition through gated networks and updating student knowledge state. Experimental results on two real datasets show our method consistently outperforms nine baseline models, improving AUC metrics by 2% to 10% while effectively addressing cold-start problems and enhancing model interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DDKT (Dual-channel Difficulty-aware Knowledge Tracing), a novel framework for knowledge tracing that integrates large language models (LLMs) to assess question difficulty and combines it with difficulty bias-aware algorithms and student mastery algorithms. DDKT addresses limitations in existing KT models related to cold-start problems, unclear personalized modeling, and lack of interpretability. Key innovations include: (1) Difficulty Balance Perception Sequence (DBPS) to measure gaps between LLM-assessed difficulty, statistical difficulty, and student-perceived difficulty; (2) Difficulty Mastery Ratio (DMR) for precise modeling of student mastery levels across difficulty zones; and (3) a knowledge state update mechanism implementing personalized knowledge acquisition. The framework uses a gated Transformer architecture. Experimental results on two real-world datasets demonstrate superior performance compared to baseline models, improving AUC and addressing cold-start issues while enhancing model interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents some interesting ideas, but the overall novelty is somewhat incremental. The integration of LLMs for assessing question difficulty is a promising direction, but the use of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) isn't groundbreaking in itself. The combination of objective and subjective difficulty assessments via attention mechanisms, while potentially useful, builds upon existing attention based KT models. The proposed DMR and DDAI are the most novel parts. Whether they are sufficiently novel is up to the reviewer.

*   **Strength:** The explicit modeling of student difficulty perception and the attempt to bridge the gap between objective and subjective assessments is a valuable contribution. The authors make a clear attempt to address the "black box" nature of deep learning KT models by trying to provide more interpretable factors. The DDAI metric could provide more personalized feedback and analysis of student understanding.
*   **Weakness:** While the components are integrated into a cohesive framework, each individual component's novelty is moderate. The core innovation lies in their *combination*. There are a lot of moving parts that are not clearly validated through rigorous ablations.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses crucial challenges in the knowledge tracing field, particularly regarding personalization, interpretability, and cold-start problems. The empirical results demonstrate consistent improvements over multiple baselines, suggesting the DDKT framework is effective in practice. The improvements, while significant, are incremental.

*   **Strength:** The paper's focus on incorporating subjective student perceptions aligns with a move towards more human-centered educational technologies. The enhanced interpretability could allow teachers to use such models more effectively. Performance improvements (2% to 8% improvement in AUC) across different datasets are positive.
*   **Weakness:** The reliance on LLMs introduces a practical challenge - LLMs are computationally expensive. Deploying DDKT on a large scale could be costly and time-consuming. The experimental setup relies on two datasets. It's important to demonstrate the generalization ability of the model across other educational domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a decent contribution to the field of knowledge tracing, but it does not represent a breakthrough. The individual components possess limited novelty, and the significance is limited by implementation concerns related to LLMs. The model seems to have too many components without clear justification for each one. Overall, the strengths are mainly with the integrated framework. Thus,

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Picking the Cream of the Crop: Visual-Centric Data Selection with Collaborative Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19917v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Liu, Yunxin Li, Baotian Hu, Wenhan Luo, Yaowei Wang, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: To improve Multimodal Large Language Models' (MLLMs) ability to process images and complex instructions, researchers predominantly curate large-scale visual instruction tuning datasets, which are either sourced from existing vision tasks or synthetically generated using LLMs and image descriptions. However, they often suffer from critical flaws, including misaligned instruction-image pairs and low-quality images. Such issues hinder training efficiency and limit performance improvements, as models waste resources on noisy or irrelevant data with minimal benefit to overall capability. To address this issue, we propose a \textbf{Vi}sual-Centric \textbf{S}election approach via \textbf{A}gents Collaboration (ViSA), which centers on image quality assessment and image-instruction relevance evaluation. Specifically, our approach consists of 1) an image information quantification method via visual agents collaboration to select images with rich visual information, and 2) a visual-centric instruction quality assessment method to select high-quality instruction data related to high-quality images. Finally, we reorganize 80K instruction data from large open-source datasets. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ViSA outperforms or is comparable to current state-of-the-art models on seven benchmarks, using only 2.5\% of the original data, highlighting the efficiency of our data selection approach. Moreover, we conduct ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of each component of our method. The code is available at https://github.com/HITsz-TMG/ViSA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViSA (Visual-Centric Selection via Agents Collaboration), a novel framework for selecting high-quality visual instruction data to improve the training efficiency of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  ViSA uses a multi-agent approach to quantify both image informativeness and instruction relevance. Image informativeness is assessed using segmentation complexity and object alignment scores, alongside a diversity perspective evaluation involving state-of-the-art visual agents. Instruction relevance is quantified through prior token perplexity and image-text mutual information.  A Shapley value-based approach combines the evaluations of different agents. The authors filter a subset of the LLaVA-OneVision dataset and demonstrate that models trained on the selected data achieve comparable or superior performance to state-of-the-art models trained on significantly larger datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** The paper tackles a fundamental issue in MLLM training - the presence of noisy and irrelevant data.  This is a well-recognized bottleneck that limits the efficiency and effectiveness of MLLM training.
    *   **Novelty of the approach:** The use of a multi-agent collaborative framework for visual data selection is a novel concept, particularly in the context of MLLMs.  The paper combines a diverse set of visual agents to assess general visual elements as well as diverse image-specific features. Explicit image informativeness evaluation is the focus and makes the contribution stand out.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation, comparing ViSA-selected data against existing models on several benchmarks. Ablation studies further validate the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   **Significant efficiency gains:** The results convincingly demonstrate the efficiency of the ViSA approach.  Achieving state-of-the-art performance using only a small fraction of the data used by baselines is a strong selling point.
    *   **Positive impact on large models:** The paper demonstrates that even already well-trained large models can benefit from continued training on ViSA-selected high-quality data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited theoretical foundation:** The theoretical justification for the specific choice of image informativeness metrics (segmentation complexity, object alignment, etc.) could be stronger. While the empirical results are compelling, there is a risk of overfitting the selection criteria to the chosen benchmarks. The subjective nature of "diversity perspectives" also raises concerns regarding consistency and generalizability.
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The method's reliance on pretrained models (SAM2, DINO) for feature extraction introduces a dependence on the capabilities and biases of those models. The effectiveness of ViSA might vary with different choices of pretrained models or if applied to datasets with drastically different visual characteristics.
    *   **Selection bias**: There is a risk that the proposed method, while effective in improving benchmark scores, will lead to a lack of diversity in data and cause the resulting models to be less robust or to exhibit undesirable biases.
    *   **Lack of complexity in interaction analysis**: The dynamics and interaction between objects in the visual images are neglected. For instance, social interaction in the image is neglected, and social AI might not benefit directly from this framework.
    *   **Shapley value complexity**: There are simpler approaches to combining the scores instead of using the Shapley value, which has a performance overhead.
    *   **Specific Data filtering**: A full data distribution with and without ViSA is not provided, limiting the potential for the results.
    *   **Limited model interaction**: The models employed are typically frozen, without interaction between the models.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of multimodal learning by demonstrating the importance of data quality over quantity. The ViSA framework provides a practical and effective approach for selecting high-quality visual instruction data, which can lead to more efficient and effective training of MLLMs. The approach is potentially generalizable to other multimodal tasks and datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   This paper could influence future research on MLLM training by shifting the focus from data collection to data selection.
    *   The ViSA framework could be adopted as a standard tool for curating high-quality training datasets for MLLMs.
    *   The insights from this paper could inspire the development of new and improved image informativeness metrics.
    *   More focus on complex image features and object interaction may open up a new window for model training and tuning.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a critical problem and presenting a novel and effective solution. The experimental results are compelling, and the ablation studies provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed framework. While there are some weaknesses, particularly regarding the limited theoretical foundation and dependence on pretrained models, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Meta-Reasoner: Dynamic Guidance for Optimized Inference-time Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19918v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Sui, Yufei He, Tri Cao, Simeng Han, Bryan Hooi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on prolonged reasoning chains to solve complex tasks. However, this trial-and-error approach often leads to high computational overhead and error propagation, where early mistakes can derail subsequent steps. To address these issues, we introduce Meta-Reasoner, a framework that dynamically optimizes inference-time reasoning by enabling LLMs to "think about how to think." Drawing inspiration from human meta-cognition and dual-process theory, Meta-Reasoner operates as a strategic advisor, decoupling high-level guidance from step-by-step generation. It employs "contextual multi-armed bandits" to iteratively evaluate reasoning progress, and select optimal strategies (e.g., backtrack, clarify ambiguity, restart from scratch, or propose alternative approaches), and reallocates computational resources toward the most promising paths. Our evaluations on mathematical reasoning and puzzles highlight the potential of dynamic reasoning chains to overcome inherent challenges in the LLM reasoning process and also show promise in broader applications, offering a scalable and adaptable solution for reasoning-intensive tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a rigorous, critical evaluation of the paper "Meta-Reasoner: Dynamic Guidance for Optimized Inference-time Reasoning in Large Language Models".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Meta-Reasoner, a framework designed to optimize inference-time reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). Drawing inspiration from human meta-cognition and dual-process theory, Meta-Reasoner acts as a strategic advisor to LLMs, decoupling high-level guidance from step-by-step generation. It employs contextual multi-armed bandits to evaluate reasoning progress iteratively, select optimal strategies (backtracking, clarification, restarts), and reallocate computational resources to promising paths. Evaluations on mathematical reasoning and puzzle-solving tasks demonstrate Meta-Reasoner's ability to overcome inherent challenges in LLM reasoning, offering a potentially scalable and adaptable solution for reasoning-intensive tasks.  It boils down to LLMs being able to "think about how to think".

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of meta-reasoning for LLMs isn't entirely new. Prior work has explored iterative refinement, self-verification, and backtracking. However, this paper distinguishes itself by offering a *structured* and *dynamic* approach to meta-reasoning. The use of contextual multi-armed bandits (MABs) to select reasoning strategies based on "progress reports" is a noteworthy contribution. The decoupling of high-level guidance from low-level generation also seems novel.
    *   **Strengths:** A structured approach to meta-reasoning is better than ad-hoc approaches as it offers an organized framework for the LLM to navigate the reasoning space.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The novelty is somewhat incremental. The use of MABs in this context is more of an application of an existing technique rather than a fundamentally new invention. The benefits may also be limited to mathematical reasoning where progress is easier to quantify rather than in abstract reasoning problems.
*   **Significance:** If successfully implemented in scalable fashion, the method holds the potential to significantly enhance the efficiency and reliability of LLM reasoning, especially for complex tasks where "trial-and-error" approaches are resource-intensive and error-prone. The focus on global oversight to avoid unproductive paths is valuable.

    *   **Strengths:** Improved efficiency has immediate benefits, and also sets the stage for improved generalization.
    *   **Weaknesses:**
        *   **Reward Function Dependence:** The performance hinges critically on the design of the reward function used to train the MAB. A poorly designed reward function could lead the meta-reasoner astray, reinforcing suboptimal strategies or hindering exploration of new possibilities. This could limit the applicability of the framework to domains where progress is easily quantifiable.
        *   **Progress Report Bottleneck:** The 'progress report' summarization step could be a bottleneck. Information loss during summarization could prevent the meta-reasoner from making informed decisions. Furthermore, the process itself depends on LLM and may be inaccurate or inconsistent.
        *   **Limited Generalization:** The experiments focus primarily on mathematical reasoning. The generalizability of the approach to other reasoning domains (e.g., common sense, natural language inference) remains unclear. Mathematical domains are easier to evaluate, so this approach may not generalize well to open-ended tasks where performance is difficult to measure.
        *   **Scalability Concerns:** While claimed as a scalable solution, the framework introduces additional LLM calls (for progress reporting and strategy generation). The computational overhead of these extra calls needs to be carefully considered, especially for large-scale applications. Is the saving in the reasoning portion is substantial enough to offset costs in other modules?

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework. The experimental setup and results are adequately presented.

*   **Potential Influence:** If further validated across diverse tasks and model scales, Meta-Reasoner could inspire new research directions in meta-learning and adaptive reasoning for LLMs. It could also inform the development of more robust and efficient reasoning agents.

*   **Rigor:** The paper is rigorous in its experimental methodology and comparisons to a comprehensive set of baselines. It also provides in depth ablations to demonstrate the utility of each component of the model.

**Justification for the Score:**

Despite its clear presentation and rigorous evaluation, the paper's novelty is somewhat incremental, and its significance is contingent on addressing the challenges related to reward function design, progress report accuracy, and generalizability. The dependence on several LLM-based components also raises concerns about computational overhead and scalability. Taking these points into consideration, I would rate the novelty and significance of the paper as moderately impactful.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### DiffCSS: Diverse and Expressive Conversational Speech Synthesis with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19924v1)
- **Authors**: Weihao wu, Zhiwei Lin, Yixuan Zhou, Jingbei Li, Rui Niu, Qinghua Wu, Songjun Cao, Long Ma, Zhiyong Wu
- **Abstract**: Conversational speech synthesis (CSS) aims to synthesize both contextually appropriate and expressive speech, and considerable efforts have been made to enhance the understanding of conversational context. However, existing CSS systems are limited to deterministic prediction, overlooking the diversity of potential responses. Moreover, they rarely employ language model (LM)-based TTS backbones, limiting the naturalness and quality of synthesized speech. To address these issues, in this paper, we propose DiffCSS, an innovative CSS framework that leverages diffusion models and an LM-based TTS backbone to generate diverse, expressive, and contextually coherent speech. A diffusion-based context-aware prosody predictor is proposed to sample diverse prosody embeddings conditioned on multimodal conversational context. Then a prosody-controllable LM-based TTS backbone is developed to synthesize high-quality speech with sampled prosody embeddings. Experimental results demonstrate that the synthesized speech from DiffCSS is more diverse, contextually coherent, and expressive than existing CSS systems
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DiffCSS: Diverse and Expressive Conversational Speech Synthesis with Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffCSS, a new conversational speech synthesis (CSS) framework designed to generate diverse and expressive speech. It addresses limitations in existing CSS systems, which often produce deterministic and less natural speech. DiffCSS uses a diffusion model for context-aware prosody prediction, allowing for the generation of varied prosody embeddings based on multimodal conversational context. It also employs a language model (LM)-based TTS backbone to synthesize high-quality speech with the sampled prosody.  Experiments demonstrate that DiffCSS generates speech that is more diverse, contextually coherent, and expressive compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a moderate degree of novelty.
    *   **Strengths:** The core idea of using diffusion models for context-aware prosody prediction in CSS is innovative. Integrating a diffusion model for this purpose, specifically to enhance prosody diversity in a conversational setting, is a relatively new contribution. Using a language model (LM)-based TTS backbone (ParlerTTS in this case) is a valuable step forward.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The LM-based TTS backbone itself builds upon existing advancements in text-to-speech technology. While the *combination* of diffusion-based prosody prediction and LM-based TTS is novel in the *CSS* domain, both elements are derived from existing techniques. The encoder for multimodal conversation context is based on widely used transformer architecture so the method is not introducing a novel architecture here.

*   **Significance and Impact:** The paper has the potential to be a significant contribution to the field of CSS, but the extent is arguable based on the evaluation.

    *   **Strengths:** Improving prosody diversity and contextual coherence are critical for creating more natural and engaging conversational agents. If the proposed method delivers on these promises, it could advance the state-of-the-art in CSS. The experimental results appear promising and may encourage other researchers to explore diffusion models for prosody control in TTS systems.

    *   **Weaknesses:** The experimental evaluation, while showcasing improvements over baseline, is limited. The subjective evaluations(MOS tests), though standard practice, can be subjective and not fully indicative of real-world performance. The ablation study only assesses multimodal context modality; it does not evaluate contribution of the proposed network's architecture or the diffusion model. The paper could benefit from an in-depth analysis of the effect of different hyperparameters of the diffusion process. Also, the improvement of prosody diversity comes with increased computation complexity. The paper does not address this or provide any quantification of how the complexity increases.

*   **Technical Soundness:** The approach seems technically sound. The model is reasonably well-described, and experiments appear to be fairly conducted. However, the paper would benefit from more in-depth discussions about the challenges and design choices when integrating the diffusion model and the TTS backbone.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and organized. However, some parts could be further clarified, such as the specific implementation details of the diffusion model and the rationale behind the choice of certain hyperparameters.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the identified strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7** is appropriate. While the paper presents a novel approach to enhance prosody diversity in CSS using diffusion models and combines it with a LM-based TTS backbone for improved naturalness, it relies on established technologies and would have benefited from more in-depth analysis/experiments with the framework architecture and diffusion process. The significance is apparent, but the provided evaluations are not enough to establish its significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Image Referenced Sketch Colorization Based on Animation Creation Workflow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19937v1)
- **Authors**: Dingkun Yan, Xinrui Wang, Zhuoru Li, Suguru Saito, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo, Jiaxian Guo
- **Abstract**: Sketch colorization plays an important role in animation and digital illustration production tasks. However, existing methods still meet problems in that text-guided methods fail to provide accurate color and style reference, hint-guided methods still involve manual operation, and image-referenced methods are prone to cause artifacts. To address these limitations, we propose a diffusion-based framework inspired by real-world animation production workflows. Our approach leverages the sketch as the spatial guidance and an RGB image as the color reference, and separately extracts foreground and background from the reference image with spatial masks. Particularly, we introduce a split cross-attention mechanism with LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) modules. They are trained separately with foreground and background regions to control the corresponding embeddings for keys and values in cross-attention. This design allows the diffusion model to integrate information from foreground and background independently, preventing interference and eliminating the spatial artifacts. During inference, we design switchable inference modes for diverse use scenarios by changing modules activated in the framework. Extensive qualitative and quantitative experiments, along with user studies, demonstrate our advantages over existing methods in generating high-qualigy artifact-free results with geometric mismatched references. Ablation studies further confirm the effectiveness of each component. Codes are available at https://github.com/ tellurion-kanata/colorizeDiffusion.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper and then conduct a critical evaluation, along with a score and justification.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel image-referenced sketch colorization framework designed to mimic the workflow used in professional animation studios. The framework addresses limitations of existing methods (text-guided, hint-guided, and image-referenced) by leveraging sketches as spatial guidance and RGB images as color references. The key innovation is a split cross-attention mechanism with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules, trained separately on foreground and background regions. This enables independent processing of these regions in the diffusion model, preventing spatial entanglement artifacts commonly seen in other image-referenced approaches. The paper also presents switchable inference modes for diverse use cases and demonstrates improved qualitative and quantitative results compared to existing methods, including user study results indicating a preference for their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its architectural design for sketch colorization, specifically the split cross-attention mechanism with LoRA. While image-referenced colorization and diffusion models are not new, the specific approach of decoupling foreground and background processing with tailored LoRA modules is a unique contribution. The mimicking of professional animation workflow is also presented as a novelty aspect, but this seems more of an inspiration source rather than a directly innovative methodological component. The switchable inference modes, although useful, are relatively straightforward and don't significantly increase the novelty of the overall approach.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a practical problem in animation and digital illustration. Artifact-free colorization with accurate reference adherence can significantly streamline production pipelines. If the method is robust and generalizable (something that needs further validation), it has the potential to be adopted by professional animators and artists. The user study, although limited, provides some evidence for the perceived quality and usability of the method. The code release is also positive, contributing to reproducibility and further development by the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a real-world problem with a practical focus.
    *   The split cross-attention mechanism is a technically sound and innovative solution to the spatial entanglement issue.
    *   Demonstrated improved qualitative and quantitative results.
    *   User study results provide subjective validation.
    *   Code is released.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper does not deeply explore the limitations of the approach, especially concerning failure cases. The dependency on mask quality is only briefly mentioned.
    *   The performance gain might be heavily dataset specific. The model is trained on large anime illustration data, and may not generalize as well to other artistic styles or photograph-like sketch colorization.
    *   Although the paper claims to mimic the workflow of animation production step-by-step, the degree of similarity is not high. The proposed framework is still an automatic system, and the workflow step-by-step is more of an inspiration.
    *   The user study methodology is not extensively detailed. The number of participants (40) is reasonable, but the specifics of their background, training, and evaluation criteria could be better defined.
    *   The novelty of mimicking professional workflows, while providing motivation, does not translate to a significant methodological innovation.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work could influence future research on sketch colorization, particularly in addressing spatial artifacts and improving adherence to reference images.
    *   The split cross-attention mechanism could be adapted to other image synthesis and editing tasks where separating foreground and background processing is beneficial.
    *   The paper could potentially influence the animation and digital illustration industries if the method proves to be robust and user-friendly.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to image-referenced sketch colorization with demonstrable improvements over existing methods. The split cross-attention mechanism is the core contribution and effectively tackles the spatial entanglement issue. However, the reliance on mask quality, dataset-specific training, and limited discussion of failure cases temper the overall assessment. While the paper is significant in addressing a practical problem and demonstrating improved results, some aspects are less strong than presented.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The score of 6.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field. The innovative architecture addresses a specific problem in a practical setting, leading to improved results. However, limitations in generalizability, mask dependency, and user study depth prevent a higher score. The novelty is not particularly groundbreaking but is a strong improvement on prior work. The paper's potential influence is tangible, but its widespread adoption will depend on further validation and user acceptance.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### GeoEdit: Geometric Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19953v1)
- **Authors**: Yujie Feng, Liming Zhan, Zexin Lu, Yongxin Xu, Xu Chu, Yasha Wang, Jiannong Cao, Philip S. Yu, Xiao-Ming Wu
- **Abstract**: Regular updates are essential for maintaining up-to-date knowledge in large language models (LLMs). Consequently, various model editing methods have been developed to update specific knowledge within LLMs. However, training-based approaches often struggle to effectively incorporate new knowledge while preserving unrelated general knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose a novel framework called Geometric Knowledge Editing (GeoEdit). GeoEdit utilizes the geometric relationships of parameter updates from fine-tuning to differentiate between neurons associated with new knowledge updates and those related to general knowledge perturbations. By employing a direction-aware knowledge identification method, we avoid updating neurons with directions approximately orthogonal to existing knowledge, thus preserving the model's generalization ability. For the remaining neurons, we integrate both old and new knowledge for aligned directions and apply a "forget-then-learn" editing strategy for opposite directions. Additionally, we introduce an importance-guided task vector fusion technique that filters out redundant information and provides adaptive neuron-level weighting, further enhancing model editing performance. Extensive experiments on two publicly available datasets demonstrate the superiority of GeoEdit over existing state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "GeoEdit: Geometric Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models" proposes a novel framework for updating knowledge in LLMs while preserving general knowledge. GeoEdit utilizes the geometric relationships of parameter updates from fine-tuning to distinguish between neurons associated with new knowledge updates and those related to general knowledge perturbations. It employs direction-aware knowledge identification, integrates old and new knowledge based on direction alignment, and utilizes a "forget-then-learn" strategy for conflicting knowledge.  The framework also incorporates an importance-guided task vector fusion technique. Experiments on two datasets demonstrate GeoEdit's superiority over existing methods in balancing knowledge updating and preserving general knowledge, especially in locality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of leveraging geometric relationships of parameter updates during fine-tuning for knowledge editing is reasonably novel.  The paper introduces a new concept called Geometric Knowledge Editing, which classifies neurons based on the angle between the knowledge retention direction and the knowledge updating direction. This is a good conceptual contribution. Also, they provide some algorithmic innovations such as direction aware knowledge identification, and importance guided task vector fusion,

*   **Significance:** Addressing the trade-off between knowledge updating and preserving generalization in LLMs is a significant problem. The paper offers a practical solution that demonstrates improved performance, particularly in terms of locality, compared to baseline methods like F-Learning. The experimental results indicate that GeoEdit is effective for knowledge editing.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a critical issue in LLM maintenance.
    *   The geometric approach provides a good framework and offers an intuitive understanding of how to modify knowledge inside LLMs.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate that GeoEdit performs well and outperforms existing approaches in some metrics.
    *   The ablation studies offer useful insights into the contributions of each part of the proposed method.
    *   It balances the need for new information with the need for preserving general knowledge in existing models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is limited. The approach is primarily an extension of existing fine-tuning techniques and relies on techniques such as task arithmetic and auto-encoders. The core geometric insight, while intuitive, may not be fundamentally groundbreaking.
    *   The experimental setup, while comprehensive, could benefit from a more diverse range of datasets and LLM architectures. The current experiments primarily use LLAMA variants.
    *   The reliance on geometric relationships might be sensitive to the specific training dynamics and architecture of the LLM. The paper doesn't thoroughly address the generalizability of the approach across different LLM families.
    *   The performance gains, while evident, are incremental.  It improves the locality metric by only 7.4%, compared with F-Learning.
    *   The gains are not as high as locate and edit methods like ROME.
    *   The paper relies on datasets of old and new knowledge, which might limit its general applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on knowledge editing by encouraging the exploration of geometric interpretations of parameter updates. It might also spur the development of more sophisticated methods for disentangling knowledge representations within LLMs.

**Justification:**

While the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of knowledge editing by proposing a new geometric approach and achieving performance improvements, several factors limit its overall score. The novelty, though present, is incremental. Also, there's a lack of very significant improvement over baseline methods. Also, more investigation is needed to evaluate and validate the model to see if the framework can be generalised to a variety of architectures and datasets. It does provide a new, intuitive way to update knowledge of models while maintaining what the model already knows and may inspire future research and development.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Collaborative Stance Detection via Small-Large Language Model Consistency Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19954v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Yan, Sheng Sun, Zixiang Tang, Teli Liu, Min Liu
- **Abstract**: Stance detection on social media aims to identify attitudes expressed in tweets towards specific targets. Current studies prioritize Large Language Models (LLMs) over Small Language Models (SLMs) due to the overwhelming performance improving provided by LLMs. However, heavily relying on LLMs for stance detection, regardless of the cost, is impractical for real-world social media monitoring systems that require vast data analysis. To this end, we propose \textbf{\underline{Co}}llaborative Stance Detection via Small-Large Language Model Consistency \textbf{\underline{Ver}}ification (\textbf{CoVer}) framework, which enhances LLM utilization via context-shared batch reasoning and logical verification between LLM and SLM. Specifically, instead of processing each text individually, CoVer processes texts batch-by-batch, obtaining stance predictions and corresponding explanations via LLM reasoning in a shared context. Then, to exclude the bias caused by context noises, CoVer introduces the SLM for logical consistency verification. Finally, texts that repeatedly exhibit low logical consistency are classified using consistency-weighted aggregation of prior LLM stance predictions. Our experiments show that CoVer outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple benchmarks in the zero-shot setting, achieving 0.54 LLM queries per tweet while significantly enhancing performance. Our CoVer offers a more practical solution for LLM deploying for social media stance detection.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper "Collaborative Stance Detection via Small-Large Language Model Consistency Verification (CoVer)" proposes a framework to improve stance detection by leveraging both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Instead of solely relying on expensive LLMs, CoVer first preprocesses tweets, then uses the LLM in a batched manner for reasoning and explanation generation. Finally, an SLM verifies the logical consistency between the LLM's reasoning and stance prediction. Inconsistent predictions are re-classified using consistency-weighted aggregation. The paper claims CoVer achieves better performance than state-of-the-art methods on several datasets with significantly fewer LLM queries, making it a more practical solution for social media monitoring.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The core strength of this work lies in its attempt to make LLM-based stance detection more practical for real-world applications where cost is a significant concern. Reducing the number of LLM queries per tweet is a valuable contribution. The batched approach enhances LLM efficiency.
    *   **Novelty:**  The integration of an SLM to verify the logical consistency of the LLM's reasoning is somewhat novel. Using the SLM as an explicit filter and a guide for re-classification addresses the LLM's known issues with hallucinations and outdated information. The consistency-weighted aggregation mechanism adds another layer of robustness.
    *   **Performance:**  The experimental results, showcasing improvements over existing zero-shot methods across several datasets, support the effectiveness of the proposed CoVer framework.  The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each component.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a well-defined methodology, breaking down the CoVer framework into distinct modules (Context Reconstruction, Batch Reasoning, Consistency Verification).
    *   **Code availability:** The fact that the authors provide a link to the code improves the reproducibility and verifiability of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach has merit, the individual components are not radically new. Context augmentation, LLM reasoning for stance detection, and using smaller models for verification have been explored in previous work (though perhaps not exactly in this combination and for this specific purpose). The main contribution lies in the specific workflow/architecture and evaluation of the combined approach.
    *   **Dependence on LLM Explanations:** The SLM relies on the LLM-generated explanations for consistency verification. The quality and clarity of these explanations are crucial. If the LLM explanations are poorly articulated, it would likely hinder the SLM's ability to accurately assess consistency.  The paper doesn't thoroughly analyze the robustness of this dependency.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** While experiments are done across multiple datasets, all of them are social media stance detection datasets. Broader applicability across different domains or tasks is not demonstrated.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Hyperparameters:**  The paper mentions specific values for several hyperparameters. It would benefit from further investigation on how these values were selected (i.e., ablation on the temperature hyperparameter Ts or other settings)
    *   **Limited discussion on limitations:** Although the authors are working towards resource efficiency they do not discuss any other limitations of their method.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a critical need in the field of stance detection: making LLM-based methods more accessible and practical.  By reducing LLM query costs, it paves the way for wider adoption in real-world scenarios.
    *   The idea of using a smaller, cheaper model to verify the reasoning of a larger, more powerful model has broad implications beyond stance detection and could be applied to other NLP tasks.
    *   The work highlights the importance of logical consistency in LLM predictions, which is a growing area of research.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a practical and reasonably novel approach to stance detection by combining LLMs and SLMs. The experimental results support the effectiveness of the proposed framework in reducing LLM queries while maintaining performance. However, the novelty is incremental, building upon existing techniques. The reliance on LLM-generated explanations and the dataset-specific focus are limitations. Overall, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a key challenge in LLM deployment, making it a practical and useful contribution that is sure to have an impact on the future of NLP research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Deterministic or probabilistic? The psychology of LLMs as random number generators
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19965v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Coronado-Blázquez
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed text generation through inherently probabilistic context-aware mechanisms, mimicking human natural language. In this paper, we systematically investigate the performance of various LLMs when generating random numbers, considering diverse configurations such as different model architectures, numerical ranges, temperature, and prompt languages. Our results reveal that, despite their stochastic transformers-based architecture, these models often exhibit deterministic responses when prompted for random numerical outputs. In particular, we find significant differences when changing the model, as well as the prompt language, attributing this phenomenon to biases deeply embedded within the training data. Models such as DeepSeek-R1 can shed some light on the internal reasoning process of LLMs, despite arriving to similar results. These biases induce predictable patterns that undermine genuine randomness, as LLMs are nothing but reproducing our own human cognitive biases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate random numbers. It examines the determinism and biases inherent in LLMs when prompted to produce random numbers within specific ranges, using various model architectures, numerical ranges, prompt languages, and temperature settings. The study reveals that, despite their probabilistic nature, LLMs often exhibit deterministic behavior and biases stemming from their training data. The paper analyzes the internal reasoning processes of some LLMs, the impact of prompt language on output distributions, and quantifies the degree of randomness using a custom-defined randomness index.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Investigation:** The paper presents a systematic and well-designed study. The factorial design, exploring different models, languages, ranges, and temperatures, allows for a comprehensive analysis of the factors influencing randomness in LLM outputs.
*   **Novelty in Prompt Language Analysis:** A significant strength is the inclusion of prompt language as a variable. This aspect hasn't been thoroughly explored in prior studies, making it a valuable contribution. Discovering differences based on the prompt languages is important because it can suggest a previously unconsidered potential to mitigate human biases.
*   **Analysis of Internal Reasoning:** The detailed analysis of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning provided by DeepSeek-R1 offers valuable insights into the internal decision-making processes of LLMs when faced with a randomness task. The insight about the inner monolouges being generally in English adds a layer of complexity not previously discussed.
*   **Quantifiable Metric:** Developing a "randomness index" is useful for quantifying the degree of bias and determinism. However, a better justification of the variables present in the index are necessary.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights the potential implications of deterministic biases for applications requiring genuine randomness, such as cryptography and statistical sampling.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Selection:** Although the paper tests several models, there is not explicit justification for avoiding models with larger parameter counts. Also, as this is a fast-evolving field, all tested models can be considered not current. The choice to prioritize DeepSeek's CoT reasoning is justified but comes at the expense of broader coverage, which can limit the generalizability.
*   **Defining Metric:** There are not strong enough arguments for the use of variables in the custom randomness index. There should have been tried other combinations of those variables, justifying the final form. Also, a mathematical definition is better than a functional approach.
*   **Interpretability of Internal Reasoning:** While the CoT analysis is insightful, the interpretation of internal monologues is subjective. This is generally a limitation of interpretablity studies.
*   **Lack of Novel Theoretical Contribution:** The paper primarily focuses on empirical observations and quantification. While the findings are interesting, the study does not propose any new theoretical frameworks for understanding or mitigating the observed biases.
*   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** The paper identifies the problem of deterministic biases, but does not offer concrete, immediately implementable strategies to mitigate those biases.

**Significance and Impact:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of literature exploring the limitations and biases of LLMs. The systematic approach and the analysis of prompt language provide valuable insights for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs. It also reinforces the idea that these models are only as good as the human biases present in its dataset and the necessity of studying those.

**Novelty:**

The examination of prompt language is the most significant novel aspect of this work. The systematic combination of other parameters such as model architecture and temperature are less novel, but does allow for comparing with previous research.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper provides a strong empirical exploration of a well-recognized problem with some novelty, leading to potential practical implications. However, the lack of theoretical innovation, the subjective interpretations, and limited mitigation strategies diminish its novelty and importance.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Can Large Language Models Unveil the Mysteries? An Exploration of Their Ability to Unlock Information in Complex Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19973v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Wang, Luning Zhang, Zheng Wang, Yang Zhou
- **Abstract**: Combining multiple perceptual inputs and performing combinatorial reasoning in complex scenarios is a sophisticated cognitive function in humans. With advancements in multi-modal large language models, recent benchmarks tend to evaluate visual understanding across multiple images. However, they often overlook the necessity of combinatorial reasoning across multiple perceptual information. To explore the ability of advanced models to integrate multiple perceptual inputs for combinatorial reasoning in complex scenarios, we introduce two benchmarks: Clue-Visual Question Answering (CVQA), with three task types to assess visual comprehension and synthesis, and Clue of Password-Visual Question Answering (CPVQA), with two task types focused on accurate interpretation and application of visual data. For our benchmarks, we present three plug-and-play approaches: utilizing model input for reasoning, enhancing reasoning through minimum margin decoding with randomness generation, and retrieving semantically relevant visual information for effective data integration. The combined results reveal current models' poor performance on combinatorial reasoning benchmarks, even the state-of-the-art (SOTA) closed-source model achieves only 33.04% accuracy on CVQA, and drops to 7.38% on CPVQA. Notably, our approach improves the performance of models on combinatorial reasoning, with a 22.17% boost on CVQA and 9.40% on CPVQA over the SOTA closed-source model, demonstrating its effectiveness in enhancing combinatorial reasoning with multiple perceptual inputs in complex scenarios. The code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of combinatorial reasoning in complex, multi-image visual scenarios. It argues that existing benchmarks for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often neglect this capability. The authors introduce two new benchmarks: Clue-Visual Question Answering (CVQA) and Clue of Password-Visual Question Answering (CPVQA), designed to evaluate combinatorial reasoning across multiple perceptual inputs. They also propose three plug-and-play methods to improve model performance on these tasks: contextual learning through detailed image captions, minimum margin decoding with randomness to enhance reasoning coherence, and semantic retrieval to identify relevant visual information.  Experiments reveal that current models perform poorly on these benchmarks, even the SOTA closed-source models, although the proposed methods offer some performance gains. The paper concludes that there's significant room for improvement in models' ability to integrate multiple perceptual inputs for combinatorial reasoning.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the introduction of the CVQA and CPVQA benchmarks. While visual question answering is a well-explored area, the focus on **combinatorial reasoning *across multiple* related images** within a complex scenario sets it apart. The benchmarks attempt to emulate real-world problem-solving situations where information from different sources must be integrated. The proposed methods are less novel, relying on existing techniques like contextual learning and minimum margin decoding. However, their application to this *specific combinatorial reasoning problem* with modifications (randomness generation) is a contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The paper highlights a genuine limitation of current MLLMs - their ability to combine information effectively from *multiple* visual sources for solving complex reasoning tasks. This is important because real-world scenarios frequently require such integration. The low performance of SOTA models on the new benchmarks underscores this weakness. The methods presented, although yielding improvements, still leave considerable performance gaps, suggesting that the problem is far from solved and provides ample opportunity for further research. The plug-and-play nature of the methods makes them easily adaptable by the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly defined problem:** The paper articulates the issue of combinatorial reasoning in MLLMs effectively.
    *   **Well-designed benchmarks:** The CVQA and CPVQA benchmarks represent a valuable contribution. The manual expansion and quality control help enhance robustness. The selection of "Can You Escape?" as a base image source provides a degree of realism.
    *   **Practical methods:** The proposed methods are practical and potentially generalizable, offering immediate avenues for improvement.
    *   **Ablation study:** The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contributions of each method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited method novelty:** The core techniques used (contextual learning, minimum margin decoding) are not fundamentally new. The main novelty is in their application and combination.
    *   **Modest performance improvements:** While the methods improve performance, the overall accuracy remains low, suggesting that the gap between current capabilities and the desired performance is still substantial.
    *   **Specific game domain:** The benchmarks are based on a specific game, raising concerns about generalizability. While visually realistic, the game might introduce domain-specific biases.
    *   **Reliance on manually crafted prompts:** Although the paper attempts to "bypass" prompt engineering, initial manual prompts are utilized for image captioning, semantic retrieval and the creation of calibration datasets used within the confidence measures during decoding. This undermines the degree to which the paper truly overcomes prompt engineering and the impact of said engineering.
    *   **Scalability of the benchmark construction:** Manual expansion is labor-intensive, limiting the scalability of the benchmark creation and evolution.
    *   **Comparisons across different parameter model sizes are not entirely fair:** Some models used were substantially larger than others, which gives these particular models an inherent advantage on the benchmarks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's main influence will likely stem from the introduction of the CVQA and CPVQA benchmarks. It will provide a concrete challenge and a standardized evaluation for researchers working on MLLMs. It can inform future benchmark design and motivate researchers to focus on more complex, multi-image reasoning scenarios. The proposed methods can serve as a baseline for future approaches. The benchmark will serve as a useful diagnostic tool for the vision and language community.

**Score:** 6/10

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution by highlighting a neglected aspect of MLLM performance: combinatorial reasoning across multiple related images. The new benchmarks (CVQA and CPVQA) are well-designed and address this gap, providing a valuable resource for the research community. The proposed methods offer some improvements, but they are not groundbreaking, and the performance gains are modest, with accuracy rates remaining rather low. The reliance on a game-specific domain, dependence on manual image extension techniques, and substantial reliance on manual prompt engineering limits the overall generalizability and scalability of the approach. Ultimately, the paper's primary value lies in framing the problem and providing the benchmarks, rather than offering a complete solution. The score reflects the valuable problem definition, dataset contribution, and identified limitations, balanced against the limited novelty of the methods and modest improvement in results.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Lookahead Limitation: Why Multi-Operand Addition is Hard for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19981v1)
- **Authors**: Tanja Baeumel, Josef van Genabith, Simon Ostermann
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive large language models (LLMs) exhibit impressive performance across various tasks but struggle with simple arithmetic, such as addition of two or more operands. We show that this struggle arises from LLMs' use of a simple one-digit lookahead heuristic, which works fairly well (but not perfect) for two-operand addition but fails in multi-operand cases, where the carry-over logic is more complex. Our probing experiments and digit-wise accuracy evaluation show that LLMs fail precisely where a one-digit lookahead is insufficient to account for cascading carries. We analyze the impact of tokenization strategies on arithmetic performance and show that all investigated models, regardless of tokenization, are inherently limited in the addition of multiple operands due to their reliance on a one-digit lookahead heuristic. Our findings reveal fundamental limitations that prevent LLMs from generalizing to more complex numerical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Lookahead Limitation: Why Multi-Operand Addition is Hard for LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates why large language models (LLMs) struggle with multi-operand addition, despite their impressive performance on other tasks. The authors argue that LLMs rely on a simple, one-digit lookahead heuristic when performing addition. This heuristic works reasonably well for two-operand addition but breaks down in multi-operand cases due to the more complex carry-over logic. Through probing experiments and digit-wise accuracy evaluations, the authors demonstrate that LLMs fail precisely where a one-digit lookahead is insufficient to account for cascading carries. They further analyze the impact of tokenization strategies but conclude that all investigated models are inherently limited due to their reliance on this heuristic.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper presents an interesting and well-supported explanation for a known problem: LLMs' difficulty with arithmetic. The paper's strength lies in its focused approach and clear articulation of the "one-digit lookahead heuristic." The experimental design, especially the creation of specific datasets to target different carry scenarios, is well-thought-out and effectively supports the paper's core argument. The probing experiments provide convincing evidence that LLMs do, in fact, struggle to represent future digits accurately when generating earlier digits in the addition process, particularly when a carry is involved. Further, the connection between the performance decrease with increasing operands and the limitations of the proposed heuristic is well-argued and experimentally validated.

However, the paper is not without limitations. While the proposed heuristic provides a compelling explanation, it might be an oversimplification. It's possible that other factors, such as the model's limited understanding of numerical concepts or the nuances of tokenization (despite the authors' analysis), also contribute to the problem. The analysis, while rigorous, primarily focuses on addition. The extent to which this heuristic generalizes to other arithmetic operations (subtraction, multiplication, etc.) remains an open question.

Additionally, the analysis and probing experiments don't explore the internal representation of carry in the LLM. Identifying specific neurons or circuits related to carry propagation could offer a deeper understanding of the issue.  Furthermore, it would strengthen the paper by considering alternative potential explanations or directly comparing their heuristic to these explanations.

The novelty of the paper is moderate. Several existing works have already identified LLMs' weaknesses in arithmetic tasks. However, the specific focus on the "one-digit lookahead heuristic" as a key limiting factor provides a valuable and concrete explanation. The paper moves beyond simply documenting the problem and proposes a specific, testable mechanism. The contribution does well in presenting strong empirical results to show the impact of the limitation.

Regarding significance, the paper's findings could influence future research on improving LLMs' arithmetic capabilities. The proposed heuristic provides a clear target for intervention: modifying the model architecture or training process to encourage a deeper lookahead. It might also influence the way prompting strategies and training data are designed for such models, which is a tangible impact of the research.

**In summary:** This is a well-written paper with clear contributions to the field, but there is some room for stronger analysis and broader experimentation. It effectively isolates a key limitation in LLMs' arithmetic reasoning, but further work will be needed to overcome this limitation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Erasing Without Remembering: Safeguarding Knowledge Forgetting in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19982v1)
- **Authors**: Huazheng Wang, Yongcheng Jing, Haifeng Sun, Yingjie Wang, Jingyu Wang, Jianxin Liao, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we explore machine unlearning from a novel dimension, by studying how to safeguard model unlearning in large language models (LLMs). Our goal is to prevent unlearned models from recalling any related memory of the targeted knowledge.We begin by uncovering a surprisingly simple yet overlooked fact: existing methods typically erase only the exact expressions of the targeted knowledge, leaving paraphrased or related information intact. To rigorously measure such oversights, we introduce UGBench, the first benchmark tailored for evaluating the generalisation performance across 13 state-of-the-art methods.UGBench reveals that unlearned models can still recall paraphrased answers and retain target facts in intermediate layers. To address this, we propose PERMU, a perturbation-based method that significantly enhances the generalisation capabilities for safeguarding LLM unlearning.Experiments demonstrate that PERMU delivers up to a 50.13% improvement in unlearning while maintaining a 43.53% boost in robust generalisation. Our code can be found in https://github.com/MaybeLizzy/UGBench.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've carefully analyzed the paper "Erasing Without Remembering: Safeguarding Knowledge Forgetting in Large Language Models." Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a crucial limitation in current LLM unlearning methods: they primarily focus on removing exact memorized facts, leaving paraphrased or related knowledge intact. To rigorously measure this, the authors introduce UGBENCH, a benchmark for evaluating the generalization capabilities of unlearning methods.  Their analysis using UGBENCH reveals that existing methods often fail to truly erase knowledge, as unlearned models can still recall paraphrased answers and retain target facts in intermediate layers.  To address this, the authors propose PERMU, a perturbation-based unlearning method that injects noise into subject token embeddings to disrupt factual recall and subtracts the original distribution from perturbed distribution.  Experiments demonstrate that PERMU significantly enhances the generalization capabilities of unlearning while maintaining model utility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel perspective on LLM unlearning. The insight that current methods are too literal and fail to generalize is significant and, as the authors claim, somewhat "embarrassingly simple yet critical." UGBENCH is a valuable contribution in itself, providing a standardized way to evaluate unlearning methods beyond simple accuracy metrics and incorporating generalization measures. PERMU, while building on the concept of adversarial examples, offers a novel way to *utilize* adversarial perturbations for improving unlearning instead of guarding against them. The specific use of token embedding perturbations and the distribution matching approach are reasonably novel.

**Significance:** The problem of inadequate generalization in unlearning is undeniably important.  As LLMs are deployed in sensitive applications (e.g., healthcare, finance, legal), ensuring complete removal of specific knowledge, *including* related paraphrased or subtly altered versions, is paramount.  UGBENCH provides a mechanism to highlight weaknesses of existing approaches and to guide future development of more robust unlearning algorithms. The paper demonstrates that PERMU provides a more practical and easily implementable means of improving generalization compared to exhaustively labeling rephrased or related data. The performance gains are statistically significant, which adds weight to the proposed method. The results showing that models still retain facts in intermediate layers after unlearning offers a critical insight for the field.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates a gap in existing research: the lack of generalization in unlearning.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The introduction of UGBENCH is a strong point, enabling systematic comparison of different unlearning methods across a range of generalization scenarios.
*   **Solid Empirical Results:** The experiments are well-designed, using multiple datasets and models, and demonstrating the effectiveness of PERMU compared to state-of-the-art baselines.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors delve into the underlying reasons for the failure of existing methods, providing valuable insights for future research.
*   **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is easy to follow and well-structured.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational overhead of PERMU, including training time and inference speed. While simplicity is an advantage, the practical implications of using PERMU on large-scale LLMs need to be considered.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions a noise ratio *P* is set to 0.4. A more rigorous study on its sensitivity would be needed to show the effectiveness of PERMU.
*   **Scope Limitation:** The paper focuses on factual knowledge unlearning. It does not address unlearning of more complex or nuanced types of information like biases or undesirable behaviors.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** While the paper empirically demonstrates the effectiveness of PERMU, it could benefit from a stronger theoretical justification for *why* perturbing subject tokens is so effective. While the MSM provides some insight, a deeper explanation could further strengthen the work.
*   **Single language scenario:** The paper only looks at English benchmarks. It should show the robustness of PERMU on other languages.
*   **Case Study in generation quality:** Tab. 10 shows a prediction by ULD on the Rephrased Unlearning sample which looks sensible. This challenges that existing unlearning methods are too literal and fail to generalize.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in LLM unlearning. UGBENCH can become a standard benchmark for evaluating unlearning methods, and the insights regarding knowledge retention in intermediate layers and the importance of generalization can inspire new approaches. PERMU, while not a perfect solution, demonstrates a promising path toward more robust unlearning and should stimulate further research into perturbation-based methods.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign the paper a score of 7.

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and addressing a critical limitation in current LLM unlearning methods: the lack of generalization. The UGBENCH benchmark and the PERMU algorithm are valuable contributions, supported by solid empirical evidence and analysis. However, the paper could be further strengthened by addressing the computational cost, hyperparameter sensitivity, and providing a stronger theoretical foundation, and expanding scope to cover other languages. While influential, the limitations prevent it from reaching the highest levels of novelty and potential impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### 3D-AffordanceLLM: Harnessing Large Language Models for Open-Vocabulary Affordance Detection in 3D Worlds
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20041v1)
- **Authors**: Hengshuo Chu, Xiang Deng, Xiaoyang Chen, Yinchuan Li, Jianye Hao, Liqiang Nie
- **Abstract**: 3D Affordance detection is a challenging problem with broad applications on various robotic tasks. Existing methods typically formulate the detection paradigm as a label-based semantic segmentation task. This paradigm relies on predefined labels and lacks the ability to comprehend complex natural language, resulting in limited generalization in open-world scene. To address these limitations, we reformulate the traditional affordance detection paradigm into \textit{Instruction Reasoning Affordance Segmentation} (IRAS) task. This task is designed to output a affordance mask region given a query reasoning text, which avoids fixed categories of input labels. We accordingly propose the \textit{3D-AffordanceLLM} (3D-ADLLM), a framework designed for reasoning affordance detection in 3D open-scene. Specifically, 3D-ADLLM introduces large language models (LLMs) to 3D affordance perception with a custom-designed decoder for generating affordance masks, thus achieving open-world reasoning affordance detection. In addition, given the scarcity of 3D affordance datasets for training large models, we seek to extract knowledge from general segmentation data and transfer it to affordance detection. Thus, we propose a multi-stage training strategy that begins with a novel pre-training task, i.e., \textit{Referring Object Part Segmentation}~(ROPS). This stage is designed to equip the model with general recognition and segmentation capabilities at the object-part level. Then followed by fine-tuning with the IRAS task, 3D-ADLLM obtains the reasoning ability for affordance detection. In summary, 3D-ADLLM leverages the rich world knowledge and human-object interaction reasoning ability of LLMs, achieving approximately an 8\% improvement in mIoU on open-vocabulary affordance detection tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Paper Summary:**

The paper introduces "3D-AffordanceLLM" (3D-ADLLM), a novel framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) for open-vocabulary affordance detection in 3D environments. The authors reformulate traditional affordance detection as an "Instruction Reasoning Affordance Segmentation" (IRAS) task, where the model outputs an affordance mask based on natural language instructions. 3D-ADLLM incorporates an LLM with a custom-designed decoder to generate these masks. To overcome the scarcity of 3D affordance datasets, the paper proposes a multi-stage training strategy: first, pre-training the model on a "Referring Object Part Segmentation" (ROPS) task to learn general object and part recognition, and then fine-tuning it on the IRAS task. The experiments demonstrate that 3D-ADLLM achieves a significant improvement in mIoU on open-vocabulary affordance detection tasks, showing its ability to reason and understand complex instructions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem: how to perform affordance detection in open-world scenarios, going beyond the limitations of fixed label sets. The use of LLMs to achieve this goal is a logical and promising direction, leveraging the rich world knowledge embedded in these models. The proposed IRAS task is a solid conceptual contribution as it changes the standard paradigm of simple label assignment to one of semantic reasoning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Problem Formulation:** The reformulation of affordance detection as an Instruction Reasoning Affordance Segmentation (IRAS) task is a significant contribution. It breaks away from the traditional reliance on pre-defined labels and moves towards a more flexible, language-driven approach. This is particularly important for handling the complexity and variability of real-world scenarios.
*   **Use of LLMs:** The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into 3D affordance detection is a timely and potentially impactful idea. LLMs offer the ability to understand complex instructions and reason about human-object interactions, enabling open-world reasoning.
*   **Multi-Stage Training Strategy:** The proposed multi-stage training strategy is a clever way to address the scarcity of 3D affordance datasets. By first pre-training on the ROPS task, the model gains general object recognition and segmentation capabilities, which are then transferred to the more specific affordance detection task. This approach improves the sample efficiency of the overall training process.
