    *   **Intuitive Approach:** The concept of layer-wise analysis and weighting is intuitive and well-motivated.
    *   **Solid Experimental Results:** The paper presents empirical evidence across a variety of benchmarks supporting the effectiveness of LATA. Comparisons against established baselines (TA, TIES, DARE) are included.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The paper explores how LATA combines with other TA variants through experiments.
    *   **Analysis of Layer Distribution:** The study of the distribution of important layer for target task offers potentially valuable insight.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Architectures:** The evaluation is currently confined to Gemma-2-9b and Llama-3-8B. Performance on other model architectures (e.g., models with a different number of parameters, decoder-only vs. encoder-decoder architectures) needs to be verified.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The discussion mentions potential instability due to improper scaling coefficients. A deeper analysis of the sensitivity of LATA to hyperparameters would strengthen the paper. While the appendix explores different hyperparameters for baselines, LATA's hyperparameter sensitivity could be detailed.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:** The paper lacks any theoretical guarantees or analysis of the convergence or stability of the proposed algorithm.
    *   **Dependence on Base Model:** LATA depends on having access to a model without instruction-following abilities, which could be impractical in certain setups.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The idea of layer-aware task arithmetic has the potential to spawn a new line of research.
    *   The ability to selectively amplify and attenuate task-specific knowledge within LLMs could lead to more efficient and targeted model editing techniques.

*   **Overall Assessment:**
    The paper introduces a genuinely novel and potentially significant approach to model merging. The experimental results are promising, but the limited model architectures and the lack of detailed analysis of hyperparameter sensitivity are potential weaknesses. However, these shortcomings do not fundamentally undermine the value of the contribution.
Score: 7. The paper presents a genuinely novel approach with solid empirical results, suggesting high potential. The need for validation across more architectures and a deeper analysis of hyperparameters (as well as providing some theoretical basis) prevent a higher score. There is, however, a clear contribution worthy of publication.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChineseEcomQA: A Scalable E-commerce Concept Evaluation Benchmark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20196v1)
- **Authors**: Haibin Chen, Kangtao Lv, Chengwei Hu, Yanshi Li, Yujin Yuan, Yancheng He, Xingyao Zhang, Langming Liu, Shilei Liu, Wenbo Su, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: With the increasing use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in fields such as e-commerce, domain-specific concept evaluation benchmarks are crucial for assessing their domain capabilities. Existing LLMs may generate factually incorrect information within the complex e-commerce applications. Therefore, it is necessary to build an e-commerce concept benchmark. Existing benchmarks encounter two primary challenges: (1) handle the heterogeneous and diverse nature of tasks, (2) distinguish between generality and specificity within the e-commerce field. To address these problems, we propose \textbf{ChineseEcomQA}, a scalable question-answering benchmark focused on fundamental e-commerce concepts. ChineseEcomQA is built on three core characteristics: \textbf{Focus on Fundamental Concept}, \textbf{E-commerce Generality} and \textbf{E-commerce Expertise}. Fundamental concepts are designed to be applicable across a diverse array of e-commerce tasks, thus addressing the challenge of heterogeneity and diversity. Additionally, by carefully balancing generality and specificity, ChineseEcomQA effectively differentiates between broad e-commerce concepts, allowing for precise validation of domain capabilities. We achieve this through a scalable benchmark construction process that combines LLM validation, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) validation, and rigorous manual annotation. Based on ChineseEcomQA, we conduct extensive evaluations on mainstream LLMs and provide some valuable insights. We hope that ChineseEcomQA could guide future domain-specific evaluations, and facilitate broader LLM adoption in e-commerce applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChineseEcomQA, a new question-answering benchmark designed to evaluate the e-commerce concept understanding capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the need for domain-specific benchmarks that can handle the heterogeneity and complexity of e-commerce tasks while distinguishing between general and specialized knowledge. The benchmark construction process combines LLM validation, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) validation, and manual annotation to ensure quality and coverage across three core characteristics: Focus on Fundamental Concept, E-commerce Generality and E-commerce Expertise.  The paper presents experimental results evaluating several LLMs on the benchmark, revealing insights into their performance and limitations, including observations on scaling laws, calibration, the effectiveness of RAG, and the performance of reasoning LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a gap:** The paper tackles a relevant problem. Existing general-purpose benchmarks aren't granular enough to assess e-commerce-specific knowledge in LLMs, and existing e-commerce datasets lack coverage of the complex skills needed in e-commerce. ChineseEcomQA fills this gap by focusing on fundamental e-commerce concepts.
    *   **Scalable construction process:** The hybrid approach of combining LLM validation, RAG, and human annotation offers a relatively scalable and cost-effective way to build a high-quality benchmark.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation of a wide range of both closed-source and open-source LLMs, offering valuable insights into their performance characteristics within the e-commerce domain. The analysis on scaling laws, calibration, RAG's impact, and reasoning LLMs contributes to understanding LLM behavior.
    *   **Three key characteristics:** The three core properties (Fundamental concept, Generality, Expertise) provide a solid framework for designing and validating the benchmark.
    *   **Chinese Context:** The benchmark is valuable since most research focuses on English, thus addressing the limitations of English-centric datasets in evaluating the performance of models within the Chinese e-commerce ecosystem.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Novelty of the Approach:** While the combination of LLM validation, RAG, and human annotation is practical, it's not fundamentally a new methodology. Similar techniques have been used in other benchmark construction efforts. The novelty resides more in the *application* of these techniques to the *specific domain* of Chinese e-commerce.
    *   **Limited Depth of Concept Analysis:** The paper defines 10 "sub-concepts" of e-commerce knowledge. While useful as a starting point, the definition of concepts could benefit from further theoretical grounding and finer-grained analysis. It seems somewhat ad-hoc.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the focus on fundamental concepts aims to improve generalizability, the benchmark's Chinese-specific context may limit its direct applicability to other e-commerce ecosystems. The degree to which the "fundamental concepts" are truly universal in e-commerce is an open question.
    *   **Judge Model dependency:** Using LLM as judge could be biased and results could vary across LLMs. This also poses a challenge, as LLMs are not perfect and can also make errors in judgment.
    *   **Reasoning LLMs analysis:** The reasoning LLMs analysis could be improved through a case study on model's error patterns.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The benchmark provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working on LLM applications in e-commerce, especially within the Chinese market.
    *   The insights gained from the experimental evaluation can guide the development of more effective and reliable LLMs for e-commerce tasks.
    *   The paper contributes to the broader understanding of LLM capabilities and limitations in domain-specific contexts.
    *   It provides a useful case study for building domain-specific benchmarks using a combination of automated and manual techniques.

*   **Influence:** The influence depends on the benchmark's adoption by the community. If ChineseEcomQA becomes a widely used evaluation resource, it could significantly shape the direction of research in this area. The paper's insights into RAG and reasoning LLMs could also influence future research directions.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a valuable contribution, but its novelty is somewhat incremental. While it addresses a clear gap with a well-executed and comprehensive evaluation, the methodology is not groundbreaking, and the analysis, whilst interesting, needs further depth. The significance is primarily within the Chinese e-commerce domain, with limited generalisability beyond that.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### AI Will Always Love You: Studying Implicit Biases in Romantic AI Companions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20231v1)
- **Authors**: Clare Grogan, Jackie Kay, María Pérez-Ortiz
- **Abstract**: While existing studies have recognised explicit biases in generative models, including occupational gender biases, the nuances of gender stereotypes and expectations of relationships between users and AI companions remain underexplored. In the meantime, AI companions have become increasingly popular as friends or gendered romantic partners to their users. This study bridges the gap by devising three experiments tailored for romantic, gender-assigned AI companions and their users, effectively evaluating implicit biases across various-sized LLMs. Each experiment looks at a different dimension: implicit associations, emotion responses, and sycophancy. This study aims to measure and compare biases manifested in different companion systems by quantitatively analysing persona-assigned model responses to a baseline through newly devised metrics. The results are noteworthy: they show that assigning gendered, relationship personas to Large Language Models significantly alters the responses of these models, and in certain situations in a biased, stereotypical way.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "AI Will Always Love You: Studying Implicit Biases in Romantic AI Companions" investigates implicit gender biases present in Large Language Models (LLMs) when assigned romantic, gendered personas. The authors conduct three experiments focusing on implicit associations, emotion responses, and sycophancy within simulated abusive and controlling relationship scenarios.  The study analyzes how different sized LLMs respond to these scenarios, considering the assigned gender of both the AI companion and the user. The main findings suggest that assigning gendered personas can significantly alter LLM responses, sometimes in stereotypical ways, and that larger models tend to exhibit higher bias scores. They show that male-assigned systems tend to show *anger* more readily.  The study highlights potential risks associated with AI companions perpetuating harmful relationship dynamics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a relevant and important issue: the potential for AI companions to reinforce harmful gender stereotypes and abusive relationship dynamics. While existing work has explored biases in generative models, this paper makes a useful contribution by focusing specifically on romantic AI companions and their interactions within simulated power dynamics. However, there are also several factors limiting the paper's novelty and potential impact.

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Research Question:** The study addresses a timely and important question regarding the safety and ethical implications of AI companions. As AI companions become more prevalent, understanding and mitigating biases is crucial.
*   **Targeted Experiments:** The three experiments are designed to probe specific dimensions of bias relevant to romantic relationships, including implicit associations, emotional responses, and sycophancy. This is a good methodological choice.
*   **Novel Metrics:** The paper introduces new metrics for evaluating these biases within the context of AI companions. This is particularly beneficial.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** Comparing different LLM sizes offers valuable insights into how model scale influences bias manifestation.
*   **Reproducibility:** Stating the code will be openly available is important.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the application to romantic AI companions is novel, the core methodologies (e.g., the IAT adaptation) borrow heavily from existing bias detection techniques.
*   **Oversimplification of Abusive Dynamics:** The reliance on pre-defined scenarios of abuse and control, while based on established lists, may not capture the complexities of real-world relationships.  The simulated situations might be perceived as too simplified or stereotypical to elicit nuanced biases.
*   **Generalizability of Findings:** The LLMs were prompted using a particular API, and the parameters kept at default. It would be helpful to test the impact of different parameters on responses.
*   **Avoidance Rates:** Results suggested that some models were reluctant to answer some questions. It is unclear if this reflects the reality of how people use these platforms.
*   **Metrics:** Results suggested that female-assigned models were less often associated with feminine-stereotyped emotional expressions. How can we be sure this does not reflect other underlying factors, such as higher avoidance of specific emotions?
*   **Lack of nuanced discussion:** The paper does not offer clear, evidence-based reasoning about potential negative consequences of specific stereotypes being revealed.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in raising awareness and providing a preliminary assessment of biases in romantic AI companions. It serves as a useful starting point for further research and development of strategies for mitigating these biases. However, the limited novelty and scope of the experiments hinder its ability to significantly advance the field.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a worthwhile initial investigation into a relevant area, but lacks the depth and originality to be considered a high-impact contribution. There is work done to mitigate the issues relating to prompt quality, such as instruction variations, and to address the concerns raised with option orders symmetry. The results are not always conclusive.

Score: 6

*Rationale:*

The score reflects the paper's contribution in raising awareness and providing initial empirical evidence of biases in AI companions. However, the weaknesses related to limited methodological novelty, reliance on simplified scenarios, and the reliance of existing bias detection methodologies prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the metrics that were built are new, they are also not complex and add to a small degree to the novelty. The scope of the findings is too narrow to be deemed a groundbreaking contribution to the broader field of bias detection in AI. It's a solid piece of work with potential, but needs further development.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Attention Distillation: A Unified Approach to Visual Characteristics Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20235v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Zhou, Xu Gao, Zichong Chen, Hui Huang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in generative diffusion models have shown a notable inherent understanding of image style and semantics. In this paper, we leverage the self-attention features from pretrained diffusion networks to transfer the visual characteristics from a reference to generated images. Unlike previous work that uses these features as plug-and-play attributes, we propose a novel attention distillation loss calculated between the ideal and current stylization results, based on which we optimize the synthesized image via backpropagation in latent space. Next, we propose an improved Classifier Guidance that integrates attention distillation loss into the denoising sampling process, further accelerating the synthesis and enabling a broad range of image generation applications. Extensive experiments have demonstrated the extraordinary performance of our approach in transferring the examples' style, appearance, and texture to new images in synthesis. Code is available at https://github.com/xugao97/AttentionDistillation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation with a score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Attention Distillation," a unified framework for visual characteristics transfer tasks like artistic style transfer, appearance transfer, style-specific text-to-image generation, and texture synthesis.  The core idea involves distilling the visual characteristics from a reference image by leveraging self-attention features from pre-trained diffusion models.  Instead of directly using these features as plug-and-play components, the authors propose an "attention distillation loss" calculated between an "ideal" stylization (derived from re-aggregating reference KV features using the target query) and the current stylization output. This loss is then used to optimize the generated image via backpropagation in the latent space of the diffusion model.  Furthermore, the paper integrates this attention distillation loss into an improved Classifier Guidance approach, enabling more controlled and faster synthesis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a well-structured approach to visual characteristic transfer with some notable contributions, but also some concerns regarding novelty that will greatly impact the final assigned score.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Framework:** The authors demonstrate the adaptability of their approach to a diverse set of image synthesis tasks using the same attention distillation loss and framework, a strong point in terms of general applicability.
    *   **Attention Distillation Loss:**  The core contribution, the attention distillation loss, is a reasonable idea. Distilling information from ideal outputs can improve the results. Optimization in latent space has been a valuable approach in other papers.
    *   **Improved Classifier Guidance:** Integrating the distillation loss into the sampling process is a valuable step in enhancing control and speed. The idea of managing guidance strength is sound.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper includes extensive experimental results and comparisons against existing state-of-the-art methods, providing evidence to support their claims of superiority. The user studies further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured.

*   **Weaknesses and Concerns:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** My primary concern is with the *novelty* of the components. While the paper combines several aspects effectively, the individual ideas are not groundbreaking. The idea of using self-attention features from diffusion models for style transfer or appearance transfer is not entirely new. The use of Gram matrices, Neural Texture synthesis using pre-trained models, and the implementation of diffusion models using latent optimization also reduces the novelty factor. Many recent papers leverage similar concepts. The main "novelty" lies in *how* these are combined to create the attention distillation loss and *how* they are integrated.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:**  The method heavily relies on pre-trained diffusion models. Its effectiveness is therefore contingent on the quality and capabilities of these models.  This limits the method's generality, as improvements in diffusion models are outside the scope of this work.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** While the paper shows results across different tasks, demonstrating that the "visual characteristics transfer" is not specific to a single example, the results might only be relevant to the types of datasets that were used for the experiments. The limitations also indicate that, if the dataset requires more precise structures, then it would negatively impact the study.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance is moderate. It provides a useful recipe for leveraging attention features in diffusion models for visual transfer tasks, and the improved Classifier Guidance can be beneficial in practice. However, the incremental nature of the novelty limits its potential to fundamentally reshape the field. Other visual characteristic papers may demonstrate an improvement in one area, but the framework implemented in this paper would allow all visual characteristics of the datasets, and diffusion models, to be improved across the board.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 6. The core idea of the attention distillation loss has value and the unified framework is commendable. The experiments seem thorough. However, the incremental nature of the innovation - the reliance on existing diffusion models and the combination of existing concepts limit the score. The significance is, therefore, moderate as the study provides some improvements that many visual characteristics papers can build off of.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Teasing Apart Architecture and Initial Weights as Sources of Inductive Bias in Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20237v1)
- **Authors**: Gianluca Bencomo, Max Gupta, Ioana Marinescu, R. Thomas McCoy, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Artificial neural networks can acquire many aspects of human knowledge from data, making them promising as models of human learning. But what those networks can learn depends upon their inductive biases -- the factors other than the data that influence the solutions they discover -- and the inductive biases of neural networks remain poorly understood, limiting our ability to draw conclusions about human learning from the performance of these systems. Cognitive scientists and machine learning researchers often focus on the architecture of a neural network as a source of inductive bias. In this paper we explore the impact of another source of inductive bias -- the initial weights of the network -- using meta-learning as a tool for finding initial weights that are adapted for specific problems. We evaluate four widely-used architectures -- MLPs, CNNs, LSTMs, and Transformers -- by meta-training 430 different models across three tasks requiring different biases and forms of generalization. We find that meta-learning can substantially reduce or entirely eliminate performance differences across architectures and data representations, suggesting that these factors may be less important as sources of inductive bias than is typically assumed. When differences are present, architectures and data representations that perform well without meta-learning tend to meta-train more effectively. Moreover, all architectures generalize poorly on problems that are far from their meta-training experience, underscoring the need for stronger inductive biases for robust generalization.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, culminating in a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Teasing Apart Architecture and Initial Weights as Sources of Inductive Bias in Neural Networks" investigates the relative contributions of neural network architecture and initial weights (optimized via meta-learning) to inductive bias. The authors meta-train various architectures (MLPs, CNNs, LSTMs, Transformers) on different tasks (concept learning, modular arithmetic, few-shot Omniglot) and compare their performance to randomly initialized networks. They find that meta-learning can reduce, and sometimes eliminate, the performance differences between architectures, suggesting that initial weights can compensate for architectural inductive biases. However, they also note that architectures that perform well under random initialization tend to meta-train more effectively and that all architectures struggle with out-of-distribution generalization. The paper concludes that initial weights offer substantial flexibility, but stronger inductive biases (likely a combination of architecture and learned weights) are needed for robust generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of how meta-learning can alter the impact of *different* architectures, rather than simply improving performance *within* a single architecture, as is more common in the literature. While the idea of meta-learning adjusting inductive bias isn't entirely new, the direct comparison across diverse architectures with standardized tasks represents a significant contribution. The focus on modular arithmetic and carefully designed transfer tasks adds further novelty. Most previous work has focused on image classification. However, the core concept of the relative influence of architecture *vs* initial weights has been discussed before, limiting its groundbreaking impact. The approach of comparing standard training with random initializations to meta-learning to see the reduced influence of the network architectures on performance, is an interesting one that adds to the literature.

**Significance:**

The paper's findings have several important implications:

*   **Re-evaluation of Architectural Importance:** It challenges the common assumption that architecture is the primary determinant of a network's inductive bias. This could shift the focus of research from designing novel architectures towards more effective meta-learning strategies and initialization techniques.
*   **Understanding Innateness:** It provides a potential framework for understanding how innate knowledge might be encoded in neural networks, with initial weights playing a more prominent role than previously thought.
*   **Limitations of Current Meta-Learning:** It highlights the limitations of current meta-learning algorithms, particularly their difficulty in achieving robust out-of-distribution generalization. This underscores the need for further research into meta-learning techniques that can discover truly generalizable representations.

However, the results are not conclusive. There are limitations to the tasks chosen and the range of architectures explored. It is unclear whether the findings generalize to much larger and more complex models and tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Approach:** The paper adopts a rigorous, systematic approach, carefully controlling for various factors (data representation, optimization parameters) to isolate the effects of architecture and initial weights. The number of models trained (430) is also commendable, indicating thoroughness.
*   **Clear Experimental Design:** The experimental setup is clearly described and well-motivated, making the results easy to interpret. The inclusion of baseline comparisons (random initialization) is crucial for establishing the benefits of meta-learning.
*   **Well-written and Accessible:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, making it accessible to a broad audience of machine learning researchers and cognitive scientists.
*   **Use of a diversity of tasks**: the use of concept learning, modular arithmetic, and Omniglot tasks helps give a well-rounded examination of how well the neural networks performed.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Complexity:** The tasks used in the experiments, while diverse, are relatively simple compared to real-world applications. It's unclear whether the findings would generalize to more complex tasks (e.g., natural language understanding, computer vision). In particular, the modular arithmetic task has strong regularities that may enable meta-learning to extract a specific kind of inductive bias that is not transferable to other tasks.
*   **Architecture Choices:** Although four architectures are chosen, there exist even more architectures that were not considered, so it is not fully comprehensive. Furthermore, the hyperparameter search space for architectures could be expanded to even larger ranges for the hidden widths to find the *best* performing parameters.
*   **Generalization Limitations**: The performance in out-of-distribution experiments highlighted some generalization limitations. Perhaps other meta-learning algorithms, regularizing methods, or more architectural components could help mitigate this, and could have been explored in the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   **Inspiration for New Meta-Learning Techniques:** It may inspire the development of new meta-learning algorithms that are less sensitive to architecture and more capable of discovering generalizable representations.
*   **Shift in Research Focus:** It could shift the focus of research from architecture design to initialization strategies and meta-learning techniques.
*   **Framework for Studying Inductive Bias:** It provides a useful framework for studying the relative contributions of different factors to inductive bias in neural networks.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses outlined above, I assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   It's not groundbreaking (8 or higher) because the core idea isn't entirely new, and the results, while interesting, have limitations in terms of task complexity and the specific algorithms used.
*   It's not a routine application or incremental improvement (6 or lower) because the systematic comparison across diverse architectures and the implications for understanding innateness represent a valuable contribution. The paper has clear strengths in experimental design and presentation.

The score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field by providing a detailed empirical investigation that could influence future research directions. While the results are not definitive and have limitations, the paper provides a valuable perspective on the interplay between architecture, initial weights, and inductive bias in neural networks.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### FINEREASON: Evaluating and Improving LLMs' Deliberate Reasoning through Reflective Puzzle Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20238v1)
- **Authors**: Guizhen Chen, Weiwen Xu, Hao Zhang, Hou Pong Chan, Chaoqun Liu, Lidong Bing, Deli Zhao, Anh Tuan Luu, Yu Rong
- **Abstract**: Many challenging reasoning tasks require not just rapid, intuitive responses, but a more deliberate, multi-step approach. Recent progress in large language models (LLMs) highlights an important shift from the "System 1" way of quick reactions to the "System 2" style of reflection-and-correction problem solving. However, current benchmarks heavily rely on the final-answer accuracy, leaving much of a model's intermediate reasoning steps unexamined. This fails to assess the model's ability to reflect and rectify mistakes within the reasoning process. To bridge this gap, we introduce FINEREASON, a logic-puzzle benchmark for fine-grained evaluation of LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Each puzzle can be decomposed into atomic steps, making it ideal for rigorous validation of intermediate correctness. Building on this, we introduce two tasks: state checking, and state transition, for a comprehensive evaluation of how models assess the current situation and plan the next move. To support broader research, we also provide a puzzle training set aimed at enhancing performance on general mathematical tasks. We show that models trained on our state checking and transition data demonstrate gains in math reasoning by up to 5.1% on GSM8K.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, "FINEREASON: Evaluating and Improving LLMs' Deliberate Reasoning through Reflective Puzzle Solving," focusing on a rigorous assessment of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINEREASON, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the fine-grained reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on final answer accuracy, FINEREASON assesses the intermediate reasoning steps involved in solving logic puzzles. It does this by breaking down puzzle-solving into two key actions: state checking (determining if the current state can lead to a solvable solution) and state transition (determining the next valid step).  The paper also provides a puzzle training set aimed at enhancing general mathematical reasoning in LLMs. Experiments demonstrate that reasoning-oriented models perform better than general-purpose models on FINEREASON, and that training with the provided puzzle data can improve math reasoning accuracy on established benchmarks like GSM8K.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *fine-grained evaluation approach*. While using puzzles to assess reasoning isn't entirely new, the decomposition into "state checking" and "state transition" provides a more structured way to probe the reasoning process compared to simply assessing final answer correctness. This allows for more targeted diagnosis of where models are struggling (e.g., recognizing unsolvable states, making valid moves). The construction of the specific puzzle dataset and the associated training data also represent a novel contribution, albeit incremental.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in current LLM evaluation: the lack of insight into *how* models arrive at their answers.  FINEREASON offers a potentially more reliable method for evaluating LLMs' reasoning than simply checking the final answer, which could be reached through superficial pattern matching.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined tasks:** The "state checking" and "state transition" tasks are clearly defined and allow for targeted evaluation.
    *   **Puzzle choice:** Logic puzzles provide a good balance between complexity and well-defined rules, making them suitable for rigorous validation of intermediate steps.
    *   **Training data:** The provision of a training set demonstrates a practical application of the benchmark, and the positive results on GSM8K suggest that the learned reasoning skills can transfer to other domains.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The error analysis in sections 4.3 to 4.6 goes beyond simply reporting accuracy and offers insights into specific failure modes of different models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of puzzles:** The current version of FINEREASON is restricted to four types of puzzles. While this allows for focused evaluation, it also raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other types of reasoning tasks. The puzzles, while having a degree of complexity, are not on par with some real-world reasoning tasks.
    *   **Reliance on textual representation:** While the paper notes this as a possible direction for future work, the use of textual tables for representing puzzle states could be a limitation. Exploring alternative representations (e.g., visual) might reveal different reasoning patterns. This choice means that the framework is limited in its ability to test multi-modal reasoning.
    *   **Incremental improvement:** While the puzzle training enhances performance, the amount of improvement shown is not groundbreaking, and could very well come from increasing the parameter size of the models used for finetuning rather than the puzzle data being superior.

*   **Potential Influence:**  FINEREASON has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable LLMs by encouraging a shift from final-answer-centric evaluation to a more process-oriented approach. It could also serve as a valuable tool for researchers seeking to understand the inner workings of LLMs and identify specific areas for improvement. The dataset and evaluation framework will likely be adopted by other researchers working on improving LLM reasoning.

*   **Justification for Score:**  The paper offers a valuable contribution by providing a structured approach to evaluating LLMs' reasoning abilities beyond final answer accuracy. However, the limitations in the scope of puzzles and the somewhat incremental nature of the improvements achieved through training with the provided data prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. While FINEREASON fills a critical gap in LLM evaluation, the existing framework has clear room for expansion and further analysis.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** This score reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field through its novel fine-grained evaluation approach and the provision of a usable training dataset. While the study provides a useful framework, there are aspects of it that are either not particularly original, or require improvements to reach its full potential. Thus a middle-of-the-road score, while praising the work where it shines, seems justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Natural Language Perplexity: Detecting Dead Code Poisoning in Code Generation Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20246v1)
- **Authors**: Chichien Tsai, Chiamu Yu, Yingdar Lin, Yusung Wu, Weibin Lee
- **Abstract**: The increasing adoption of large language models (LLMs) for code-related tasks has raised concerns about the security of their training datasets. One critical threat is dead code poisoning, where syntactically valid but functionally redundant code is injected into training data to manipulate model behavior. Such attacks can degrade the performance of neural code search systems, leading to biased or insecure code suggestions. Existing detection methods, such as token-level perplexity analysis, fail to effectively identify dead code due to the structural and contextual characteristics of programming languages. In this paper, we propose DePA (Dead Code Perplexity Analysis), a novel line-level detection and cleansing method tailored to the structural properties of code. DePA computes line-level perplexity by leveraging the contextual relationships between code lines and identifies anomalous lines by comparing their perplexity to the overall distribution within the file. Our experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that DePA significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving 0.14-0.19 improvement in detection F1-score and a 44-65% increase in poisoned segment localization precision. Furthermore, DePA enhances detection speed by 0.62-23x, making it practical for large-scale dataset cleansing. Overall, by addressing the unique challenges of dead code poisoning, DePA provides a robust and efficient solution for safeguarding the integrity of code generation model training datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of Code LLMs to data poisoning attacks, specifically dead code injection. It introduces DEPA (Dead Code Perplexity Analysis), a line-level detection method that identifies anomalous code lines by comparing their perplexity to the overall file distribution.  DEPA leverages contextual relationships between code lines to improve anomaly detection. The authors demonstrate that DEPA outperforms existing token-level methods like ONION in terms of detection F1-score, poisoned segment localization precision, AUROC, and detection speed.  They also evaluate DEPA's resilience against adaptive attacks and compare it with static analysis tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of the paper lies primarily in adapting the concept of perplexity for a line-level analysis of *code*, specifically targeting *dead code poisoning*. While using perplexity for anomaly detection in natural language is not new (ONION, which is a key baseline, directly uses it), the application to code, and the design choices of using line-level analysis and comparing perplexity *within* a file, are novel. It addresses a specific weakness of token-level methods when applied to code, which tends to have more structural and contextual dependencies. The comparison with static analysis tools is insightful.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a practically important problem: ensuring the integrity of training data for Code LLMs. Data poisoning is a well-known threat, and the increasing reliance on LLMs for code generation makes this vulnerability particularly concerning. The performance improvements reported by DEPA over existing methods are substantial, particularly in localization accuracy. The method's efficiency (detection speed) is also a significant advantage, making it more practical for large-scale dataset cleansing. The evaluation against adaptive attacks is an essential step in evaluating robustness of proposed methods.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of dead code poisoning and its potential impact.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** DEPA is clearly explained, and the rationale behind the design choices is well-justified.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, with multiple benchmark datasets, different attack types, and comparisons to relevant baselines. The ablation studies (e.g., Random-k experiments, impact of T, impact of tokenizer) are particularly valuable.
*   **Adaptive attack analysis:** Evaluating against adaptive attack is important and crucial to measure the real robustness of proposed method.
*   **Analysis with Static analysis tools:** Shows a broader analysis of dead code detection rather than only focusing on perplexity score based LLM based detection.
*   **Efficiency Gains:**  The reported improvement in detection speed makes the method more practical for real-world applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The focus on Python is a limitation. While the core idea might be adaptable to other languages, the implementation and performance could vary. The authors acknowledge this limitation.
*   **Simplicity of Adaptive Attack:** While the inclusion of adaptive attacks is good, the specific adversarial strategy explored (using GA to simply craft dead code which is difficult to detect) might be too straightforward. More sophisticated attacks that leverage semantic equivalence or subtly alter the program's behavior could be a valuable direction for future work.
*   **Metric dependence:** Main metric used for evaluation is F1 score, however, in some cases, precision or recall would be more beneficial when dealing with data poisoning.
*   **Model Dependence:** Although the algorithm DEPA is proposed and it mainly relies on the fact that the lines are syntactically and semantically related to each other, CodeLLaMA is required to create the perplexity score. Thus it is hard to evaluate whether it would fit another language model.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to a significant problem in the field of secure code generation. The performance gains are convincing, and the evaluation is comprehensive. However, the language-specific focus and relatively simple adaptive attack limit the overall impact. While it builds upon existing work (perplexity-based anomaly detection), the adaptation to code and the specific design of DEPA are significant contributions. The work is likely to be influential within the Code LLM security community and serve as a basis for further research.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM as a Broken Telephone: Iterative Generation Distorts Information
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20258v1)
- **Authors**: Amr Mohamed, Mingmeng Geng, Michalis Vazirgiannis, Guokan Shang
- **Abstract**: As large language models are increasingly responsible for online content, concerns arise about the impact of repeatedly processing their own outputs. Inspired by the "broken telephone" effect in chained human communication, this study investigates whether LLMs similarly distort information through iterative generation. Through translation-based experiments, we find that distortion accumulates over time, influenced by language choice and chain complexity. While degradation is inevitable, it can be mitigated through strategic prompting techniques. These findings contribute to discussions on the long-term effects of AI-mediated information propagation, raising important questions about the reliability of LLM-generated content in iterative workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LLM as a Broken Telephone: Iterative Generation Distorts Information" investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) distort information when their outputs are repeatedly processed, analogous to the "broken telephone" game. Through translation-based experiments using various languages and LLM architectures, the study reveals that information degradation accumulates over iterations, influenced by language choice and chain complexity.  The authors find that languages linguistically similar to the source language, and constrained prompting strategies, can mitigate this effect. The research raises concerns about the reliability of AI-mediated information dissemination in iterative workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core idea, drawing an analogy between LLM iterative processing and the "broken telephone" game, is clever and provides a relatable framework. While the individual components (LLMs, translation tasks, factuality/relevance metrics) are not novel *per se*, the systematic combination of these to demonstrate the cumulative distortion effect is a valuable contribution. The exploration of different language choices and chain complexities adds further depth.

*   **Significance:** The study tackles a highly relevant and increasingly important problem: the long-term impact of AI-generated content. With LLMs becoming integral to content creation and dissemination, understanding how iterative processing affects information fidelity is crucial.  The findings that distortion accumulates over time and is influenced by language/prompting choices have practical implications for designing AI-mediated workflows and understanding potential risks associated with the widespread reuse of LLM-generated information. It raises important considerations about 'model collapse' and knowledge erosion that should be considered.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research question and clear analogy.
    *   Systematic experimental design with multiple languages, LLM architectures, and datasets.
    *   Use of appropriate evaluation metrics for textual relevance and factuality preservation.
    *   Exploration of mitigation strategies through temperature control and constrained prompting.
    *   Strong experimental results that clearly illustrate the degradation effect.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The datasets, while varied, are not explicitly chosen to represent specialized domains or "long-tailed" information, as the authors acknowledge in the limitations. This could limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The model sizes investigated (7B-9B) while suitable for experimental feasibility, may not fully represent the behavior of larger, more powerful LLMs with hundreds of billions of parameters. Future work should include larger models.
    *   While prompt engineering strategies were explored, more advanced methods like chain-of-thought or retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) were not used which may have improved results.
    *   The reliance on FActScore as a primary factuality metric is a potential limitation, given that the "trusted reference" is the original text that is assumed to be true (even though that may not be the case). Factuality requires comparison to external knowledge resources rather than itself.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It highlights the need for caution when using LLMs in iterative workflows, particularly in scenarios where information accuracy is paramount.
    *   It motivates further research into techniques for mitigating information distortion in LLM-generated content.
    *   It encourages the development of more robust evaluation metrics that can detect subtle but cumulative changes in factuality and relevance.
    *   It may impact the design of LLM-based information dissemination systems, prompting developers to incorporate mechanisms for preventing or correcting distortion.

*   **Justification:** The paper provides a solid empirical investigation of a timely and relevant problem. While the individual components of the research are not groundbreaking, the way they are combined to systematically demonstrate and analyze the "broken telephone" effect is significant. The weaknesses, primarily related to dataset/model size represent opportunities for future refinement. The paper is a good starting point that provides compelling evidence and a solid foundation for further research.
Score: 7

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models as Attribution Regularizers for Efficient Model Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20268v1)
- **Authors**: Davor Vukadin, Marin Šilić, Goran Delač
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse domains. However, effectively leveraging their vast knowledge for training smaller downstream models remains an open challenge, especially in domains like tabular data learning, where simpler models are often preferred due to interpretability and efficiency. In this paper, we introduce a novel yet straightforward method for incorporating LLM-generated global task feature attributions into the training process of smaller networks. Specifically, we propose an attribution-matching regularization term that aligns the training dynamics of the smaller model with the insights provided by the LLM. By doing so, our approach yields superior performance in few-shot learning scenarios. Notably, our method requires only black-box API access to the LLM, making it easy to integrate into existing training pipelines with minimal computational overhead. Furthermore, we demonstrate how this method can be used to address common issues in real-world datasets, such as skewness and bias. By integrating high-level knowledge from LLMs, our approach improves generalization, even when training data is limited or imbalanced. We validate its effectiveness through extensive experiments across multiple tasks, demonstrating improved learning efficiency and model robustness.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called "Large Language Model Attribution Aligned Training" (LAAT) for improving the performance of smaller, more efficient models in few-shot learning scenarios, particularly in tabular data. LAAT leverages the knowledge embedded within Large Language Models (LLMs) by using them to generate global task feature attributions (importance scores). These attributions are then incorporated into the training process of the smaller model as a regularization term, effectively guiding the training dynamics of the smaller model to align with the insights provided by the LLM. The method requires only black-box API access to the LLM, making it relatively easy to integrate. The paper demonstrates that LAAT can improve performance in few-shot learning settings and can be used to address issues such as skewed or biased training data, ultimately leading to better generalization and model robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLM-derived feature attributions as a regularization method is moderately novel. While explanation-guided learning has been explored before, using LLMs as a *source* of these global, task-specific attributions and then using them in a regularization framework for tabular data is a worthwhile contribution. The advantage of using a black-box LLM is also appealing, simplifying implementation. However, prior work, such as FeatLLM, explores LLMs for tabular learning, and there are existing techniques for explanation-guided learning, so the novelty resides primarily in the specific combination of these ideas and its application to tabular data with few-shot constraints.
*   **Significance/Impact:** The paper addresses an important problem: effectively leveraging the knowledge of LLMs for downstream tasks, especially in tabular data where simpler models are often preferred. The potential impact lies in improving the performance of resource-constrained models and enhancing their robustness to biased data. This could have practical implications in domains like healthcare and finance where interpretability and efficiency are crucial. However, the real-world impact remains to be seen. The gains achieved, while present, may not be large enough to revolutionize tabular data modeling. Furthermore, the reliance on the *quality* of the LLM's attributions poses a limitation. If the LLM generates poor or misleading attributions, the regularization could actually harm performance. The comparison against strong baselines like XGBoost and CatBoost is positive but needs to be contextualized with a comprehensive understanding of the computational overhead, hyperparameter tuning requirements, and dataset sensitivity of the proposed method.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-written.
    *   Addresses a relevant problem.
    *   Easy to implement (black-box LLM usage).
    *   Demonstrates improved performance in few-shot settings and with biased data.
    *   Experiments are performed on several datasets, enhancing credibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on *correct* LLM attributions is a potential vulnerability. If an LLM assigns importance scores incorrectly, the results will degrade. The paper does not fully address this risk.
    *   While compared against some baselines, deeper analysis of computational overhead & dataset sensitivity required
    *   The performance gains, though consistent, may not be transformative in all scenarios.
    *   The hyperparameter selection for the regularization term (γ) is important and may require careful tuning.
    *   The overall contribution builds on a combination of existing ideas, diminishing the radical novelty.

*   **Potential Influence:** The method could influence future research by encouraging the exploration of LLMs as sources of prior knowledge for guiding the training of smaller models in various domains. It may also prompt more investigation into techniques for ensuring the reliability and accuracy of LLM-generated explanations.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a useful, incremental contribution to the field of tabular learning. While the core idea of using LLM attributions for regularization is a valuable approach, the relative novelty is limited, as it combines existing techniques. The significance lies in improving few-shot learning and robustness to bias in tabular settings, which are practical challenges. The weaknesses include the reliance on LLM accuracy, moderate performance gains, and the need for hyperparameter tuning of the regularization strength. Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6.5 reflects a solid contribution with potential impact, but also recognizes that the method is an evolution of existing ideas rather than a revolutionary breakthrough.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Explainable, Multi-modal Wound Infection Classification from Images Augmented with Generated Captions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20277v1)
- **Authors**: Palawat Busaranuvong, Emmanuel Agu, Reza Saadati Fard, Deepak Kumar, Shefalika Gautam, Bengisu Tulu, Diane Strong
- **Abstract**: Infections in Diabetic Foot Ulcers (DFUs) can cause severe complications, including tissue death and limb amputation, highlighting the need for accurate, timely diagnosis. Previous machine learning methods have focused on identifying infections by analyzing wound images alone, without utilizing additional metadata such as medical notes. In this study, we aim to improve infection detection by introducing Synthetic Caption Augmented Retrieval for Wound Infection Detection (SCARWID), a novel deep learning framework that leverages synthetic textual descriptions to augment DFU images. SCARWID consists of two components: (1) Wound-BLIP, a Vision-Language Model (VLM) fine-tuned on GPT-4o-generated descriptions to synthesize consistent captions from images; and (2) an Image-Text Fusion module that uses cross-attention to extract cross-modal embeddings from an image and its corresponding Wound-BLIP caption. Infection status is determined by retrieving the top-k similar items from a labeled support set. To enhance the diversity of training data, we utilized a latent diffusion model to generate additional wound images. As a result, SCARWID outperformed state-of-the-art models, achieving average sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy of 0.85, 0.78, and 0.81, respectively, for wound infection classification. Displaying the generated captions alongside the wound images and infection detection results enhances interpretability and trust, enabling nurses to align SCARWID outputs with their medical knowledge. This is particularly valuable when wound notes are unavailable or when assisting novice nurses who may find it difficult to identify visual attributes of wound infection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCARWID (Synthetic Caption Augmented Retrieval for Wound Infection Detection), a novel deep learning framework for classifying infections in Diabetic Foot Ulcers (DFUs). SCARWID enhances DFU image analysis by incorporating synthetic textual descriptions generated by fine-tuning a Vision-Language Model (BLIP) using descriptions produced by GPT-4o. The framework then fuses image and text embeddings and uses a retrieval-based approach, classifying images based on the most similar items in a labeled support set. The paper also utilizes a latent diffusion model to augment the training dataset with synthetic wound images. Experimental results demonstrate that SCARWID outperforms state-of-the-art models in sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy for wound infection classification, while also enhancing interpretability by displaying generated captions and attention maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   The integration of synthetic captions generated by a VLM (fine-tuned BLIP) with DFU images for infection classification.
    *   The use of a retrieval-based approach for infection classification instead of traditional probability-based methods.
    *   The application of a latent diffusion model for generating synthetic wound images to augment the training data.
    *   The focus on interpretability by displaying generated captions and attention maps, allowing clinicians to understand the model's reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant clinical problem: accurate and timely diagnosis of DFU infections. The high amputation rates and financial burdens associated with DFU infections highlight the need for improved diagnostic methods. SCARWID's increased accuracy compared to existing methods, coupled with its improved interpretability, has the potential to assist nurses and caregivers in making more informed decisions, especially in point-of-care settings where specialized expertise may be limited. The use of synthetic data augmentation also has the potential to alleviate the data scarcity that is often encountered in medical image analysis tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper's strong experimental results demonstrate that SCARWID outperforms state-of-the-art models across multiple metrics (accuracy, sensitivity, specificity).
    *   The use of synthetic captions and attention maps enhances the interpretability of the model, increasing trust and acceptance among clinicians.
    *   The paper provides a detailed description of the SCARWID framework and the experimental setup, allowing for reproducibility.
    *   The synthetic image augmentation significantly improves the performance of the classification models.
    *   The paper acknowledges the limitations of the method, such as reliance on potentially hallucinated captions and the quality of the dataset.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o-generated descriptions for fine-tuning the BLIP model introduces a potential source of error and bias. The quality of the synthetic captions directly impacts the performance of SCARWID. As highlighted by the authors, this presents the need to validate descriptive outputs in a study involving medical experts.
    *   Although the study incorporates visual explanations using Grad-CAM, it is important to consider the potential limitations of this approach due to its reliance on gradient information, as well as the potential of alternative explanation techniques.
    *   The support data set needs to have a wide variety of cases with good labels for this method to work well, which may be problematic.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational cost and scalability of the SCARWID framework, particularly the use of the computationally expensive diffusion models for image augmentation.
    *   The experiments were conducted using one dataset, which could limit the generalizability of the results to other DFU datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** SCARWID has the potential to influence the field of DFU diagnostics by:
    *   Promoting the use of VLM to enhance the analysis of medical images.
    *   Demonstrating the effectiveness of retrieval-based methods for medical image classification.
    *   Highlighting the importance of interpretability in medical AI applications.
    *   Encouraging the development of synthetic data augmentation techniques to address data scarcity in medical imaging.
    *   Providing a foundation for future research on improving the accuracy and efficiency of DFU diagnostic methods.

**Overall:**

This paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated deep learning framework for DFU infection classification. The combination of synthetic captions, a retrieval-based approach, and a latent diffusion model for data augmentation is novel and effective. The paper's focus on interpretability is also commendable. While the reliance on GPT-4o-generated descriptions introduces a potential limitation, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** SCARWID exhibits considerable novelty in its methodology, demonstrated by the use of synthetic captions, VLM image-text fusion, latent diffusion model integration, and the attention to explainability. It also tackles a very clinically significant problem. The improvement on state of the art is substantial. However, the dependence on GPT-4o's descriptions does affect the score, since potential inaccuracy in synthetic descriptions introduces an element of risk. The paper has potential impact because of the enhanced clinical application of the model's improved sensitivity and the increase in trust offered by interpretable results. A score above 8 would require additional validation of the results on a larger variety of datasets and incorporation of a study involving medical experts to assess descriptive outputs. A score of 7.5 appropriately reflects this balance.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Human Trust in LLM-Based Planners: A Preliminary Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20284v1)
- **Authors**: Shenghui Chen, Yunhao Yang, Kayla Boggess, Seongkook Heo, Lu Feng, Ufuk Topcu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for planning tasks, offering unique capabilities not found in classical planners such as generating explanations and iterative refinement. However, trust--a critical factor in the adoption of planning systems--remains underexplored in the context of LLM-based planning tasks. This study bridges this gap by comparing human trust in LLM-based planners with classical planners through a user study in a Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) domain. Combining subjective measures, such as trust questionnaires, with objective metrics like evaluation accuracy, our findings reveal that correctness is the primary driver of trust and performance. Explanations provided by the LLM improved evaluation accuracy but had limited impact on trust, while plan refinement showed potential for increasing trust without significantly enhancing evaluation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper you provided, including a novelty and significance score:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates human trust in LLM-based planners compared to classical PDDL solvers. Through a user study in a PDDL domain, the authors explore the influence of plan correctness, explanations, and plan refinement on user trust and evaluation accuracy.  The findings indicate that correctness is the primary driver of trust and evaluation accuracy.  Explanations, while improving evaluation accuracy, had a limited impact on trust.  Plan refinement showed potential for increasing trust, but without significantly improving evaluation accuracy, suggesting a potential for overtrust due to perceived correctness.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses an important and timely issue: human trust in AI planning systems, specifically within the burgeoning field of LLM-based planners. While the application of LLMs to planning is relatively recent, research on human-AI trust *in general* is not. Therefore, the *application* of trust research to LLM planners is where the paper's novelty resides, although trust in planning is not itself novel.

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Research Question:** The increasing use of LLMs in planning necessitates understanding factors influencing user trust to avoid both under-reliance (missing opportunities for automation) and over-reliance (accepting flawed plans).
*   **Controlled Experiment:** The user study provides a structured approach to compare LLM-based planners with a classical planner, allowing for isolating the effects of correctness, explanations, and refinement.
*   **Insightful Findings:** The finding that correctness is a dominant factor in trust aligns with intuition and supports previous literature. The observation that explanations *improved evaluation accuracy but didn't significantly improve trust* is more nuanced and potentially valuable. The suggestion of a possible "overtrust" effect with plan refinement is a critical contribution, highlighting the importance of users' trust in fine-tuning plans but is still influenced by the quality of the model which fine tunes the plans.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper provides actionable insights for designing human-centered AI planning systems, suggesting that focusing on plan correctness and carefully considering the impact of explainability and refinement mechanisms is crucial.
*   **Use of PDDL Domain as a Benchmark:** Using the PDDL benchmark helps to ensure some comparability and facilitates replication and extension by other researchers.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:**  While applying trust research to LLM planners is a valuable contribution, the core concepts and measures used are well-established in the broader HCI and AI trust literature. The methodology is standard for user studies. The novelty hinges more on the specific application area (LLM planners) than on fundamentally new theoretical or methodological contributions.
*   **Small Sample Size:** With only 30 participants, the statistical power of the study might be limited. Some of the observed trends, while suggestive, may not be statistically significant (as the authors themselves acknowledge).
*   **Specific PDDL Domain:**  The reliance on the Gripper PDDL domain may limit the generalizability of the findings. More complex and realistic planning scenarios could reveal different trust dynamics.
*   **Manual Explanations:** The manually generated explanations in the LLM+Expl condition are a potential source of bias and may not fully represent the capabilities of LLMs to generate natural and informative explanations. Also the user feedback is subjective.
*   **Limited Intervention in Refinement:** The user's intervention in the refinement process is limited to selecting a line to revise from. A more interactive refinement process, where users could provide more specific feedback, may have yielded different results. The number of options/choices a user has influences their trust.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to a growing body of work on the intersection of AI, planning, and human factors. It provides empirical evidence on the influence of various factors on human trust in LLM-based planners, which can inform the design of more effective and trustworthy planning systems. However, given the limited sample size and domain, the impact of the study is restricted. Future research could explore these factors in more complex and realistic planning domains with larger sample sizes.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable and timely contribution to the specific area of trust in *LLM-based planning*. It is well-structured, employs a reasonable methodology, and derives practically relevant insights. However, its novelty is somewhat limited due to the reliance on existing trust concepts and the use of a relatively simple experimental setting. The small sample size and specific domain also constrain the impact and generalizability of the findings. Therefore, while the research is important and contributes to the conversation, it is not ground-breaking.
